In {@{[physics](physics.md), __Lagrangian mechanics__}@} is {@{an alternate formulation of [classical mechanics](classical%20mechanics.md) founded on the [d'Alembert principle](d'Alembert's%20principle.md) of virtual work}@}. It was introduced by {@{the Italian-French mathematician and astronomer [Joseph-Louis Lagrange](Joseph-Louis%20Lagrange.md)}@} in his {@{presentation to the Turin Academy of Science in 1760}@}<sup>[\[1\]](#^ref-1)</sup> culminating in his {@{1788 grand opus, _[Mécanique analytique](Mécanique%20analytique.md)_}@}.<sup>[\[2\]](#^ref-2)</sup> {@{Lagrange's approach}@} greatly simplifies {@{the analysis of many problems in mechanics}@}, and it had {@{crucial influence on other branches of physics}@}, including {@{relativity and quantum field theory}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:33:47.058Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:33:47.058Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:54:01.168Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:54:01.168Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:04:00.263Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:04:00.263Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:11.225Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:11.225Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:27.827Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:27.827Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:25:24.246Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:25:24.246Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:38.465Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:38.465Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:40:42.765Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:40:42.765Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:59.494Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:59.494Z-->

{@{Lagrangian mechanics}@} describes {@{a mechanical system as a pair \(_M_, _L_\)}@} consisting of {@{a [configuration space](configuration%20space%20(physics).md) _M_ and a smooth function $L$ within that space called a ___Lagrangian___}@}. For {@{many systems, _L_ = _T_ − _V_}@}, where {@{_T_ and _V_ are the [kinetic](kinetic%20energy.md) and [potential](potential%20energy.md) energy of the system, respectively}@}.<sup>[\[3\]](#^ref-3)</sup> <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:55.544Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:55.544Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:25:49.864Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:25:49.864Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:20.576Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:20.576Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:12.131Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:12.131Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:21:21.051Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:21:21.051Z-->

{@{The stationary action principle}@} requires that {@{the [action functional](action%20(physics).md#action%20(functional)) of the system derived from _L_}@} must {@{remain at a [stationary point](stationary%20point.md) \(specifically, a [maximum](maximum%20and%20minimum.md), [minimum](maximum%20and%20minimum.md), or [saddle](saddle%20point.md) point\) throughout the time evolution of the system}@}. {@{This constraint}@} allows {@{the calculation of the equations of motion of the system}@} using Lagrange's equations.<sup>[\[4\]](#^ref-4)</sup> <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:18.110Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:18.110Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:19.334Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:19.334Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:14.914Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:14.914Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:08:07.751Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:08:07.751Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:31:54.942Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:31:54.942Z-->

## introduction

> {@{![Bead on wire. Shape of wire given by equation f\(x,y\)=0, the constraint force is C, and the one degree of freedom can be described by one generalized coordinate \(here the arc length s\). Notice the initial position of the wire can lead to different motions.](../../archives/Wikimedia%20Commons/Bead%20on%20wire%20constraint.svg)}@}
>
> {@{Bead constrained}@} to {@{move on a frictionless wire}@}. {@{The wire}@} exerts {@{a reaction force __C__ on the bead to keep it on the wire}@}. {@{The non-constraint force __N__}@} in this case is {@{gravity}@}. Notice {@{the initial position of the bead on the wire}@} can lead to {@{different motions}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:55.360Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:55.360Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:34:45.289Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:34:45.289Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:42:28.744Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:42:28.744Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:44.709Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:44.709Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:28:23.213Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:28:23.213Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:31:14.616Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:31:14.616Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:33:51.437Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:33:51.437Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:19.646Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:19.646Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:53.046Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:53.046Z-->

<!-- markdownlint MD028 -->

> {@{![Simple pendulum. Since the rod is rigid, the position of the bob is constrained according to the equation f\(x,y\)=0, the constraint force is C, and the one degree of freedom can be described by one generalized coordinate \(here the angle theta\).](../../archives/Wikimedia%20Commons/Pendulum%20constraint.svg)}@}
>
> {@{Simple pendulum}@}. Since {@{the rod is rigid}@}, {@{the position of the bob}@} is {@{constrained according to the equation _f_\(_x_, _y_\) = 0}@}, {@{the constraint force __C__}@} is {@{the tension in the rod}@}. Again {@{the non-constraint force __N__}@} in this case is {@{gravity}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:27:07.563Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:27:07.563Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:20:31.037Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:20:31.037Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:22.017Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:22.017Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:42.273Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:42.273Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:07.184Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:07.184Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:49.618Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:49.618Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:01.683Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:01.683Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:39.261Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:39.261Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:31.223Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:31.223Z-->

{@{Newton's laws and the concept of forces}@} are {@{the usual starting point for teaching about mechanical systems}@}.<sup>[\[5\]](#^ref-5)</sup> {@{This method}@} works {@{well for many problems}@}, but for {@{others the approach is nightmarishly complicated}@}.<sup>[\[6\]](#^ref-6)</sup> For example, in {@{calculation of the motion of a [torus](torus.md)}@} rolling on {@{a horizontal surface with a pearl sliding inside}@}, {@{the time-varying constraint forces}@} like {@{the [angular velocity](angular%20velocity.md) of the torus and the motion of the pearl in relation to the torus}@} made it {@{difficult to determine the motion of the torus with Newton's equations}@}.<sup>[\[7\]](#^ref-7)</sup> {@{Lagrangian mechanics}@} adopts {@{energy rather than force as its basic ingredient}@},<sup>[\[5\]](#^ref-5)</sup> leading to more {@{abstract equations capable of tackling more complex problems}@}.<sup>[\[6\]](#^ref-6)</sup> <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:29:46.343Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:29:46.343Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:30.479Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:30.479Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:38:03.222Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:38:03.222Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:29:43.204Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:29:43.204Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:27:03.344Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:27:03.344Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:40:47.528Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:40:47.528Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:18:03.333Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:18:03.333Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:43:46.760Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:43:46.760Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:08.484Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:08.484Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:09.915Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:09.915Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:50.082Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:50.082Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:37.712Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:37.712Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:15.252Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:15.252Z-->

Particularly, {@{Lagrange's approach}@} was to {@{set up independent [generalized coordinates](generalized%20coordinate.md) for the position and speed of every object}@}, which allows {@{the writing down of a general form of the Lagrangian}@} \({@{total kinetic energy minus potential energy of the system}@}\) and summing {@{this over all possible paths of motion of the particles}@} yielded {@{a formula for the 'action'}@}, which he {@{minimized to give a generalized set of equations}@}. {@{This summed quantity}@} is {@{minimized along the path that the particle actually takes}@}. {@{This choice}@} eliminates {@{the need for the constraint force to enter into the resultant generalized [system of equations](system%20of%20equations.md)}@}. There are {@{fewer equations}@} since one is {@{not directly calculating the influence of the constraint on the particle at a given moment}@}.<sup>[\[7\]](#^ref-7)</sup> <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:09.412Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:09.412Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:50:21.428Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:50:21.428Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:28.086Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:28.086Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:41.468Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:41.468Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:40:27.903Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:40:27.903Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:36:52.014Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:36:52.014Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:40.518Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:40.518Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:48:30.410Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:48:30.410Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:28:02.080Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:28:02.080Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:05.983Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:05.983Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:18:02.293Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:18:02.293Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:29:31.236Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:29:31.236Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:08:45.845Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:08:45.845Z-->

For {@{a wide variety of physical systems}@}, if {@{the size and shape of a massive object are negligible}@}, it is a useful simplification to {@{treat it as a [point particle](point%20particle.md)}@}. For {@{a system of _N_ point particles with [masses](mass.md) _m_<sub>1</sub>, _m_<sub>2</sub>, ..., _m<sub>N</sub>_}@}, {@{each particle}@} has {@{a [position vector](position%20vector.md), denoted __r__<sub>1</sub>, __r__<sub>2</sub>, ..., __r__<sub>_N_</sub>}@}. {@{[Cartesian coordinates](Cartesian%20coordinates.md)}@} are {@{often sufficient}@}, so {@{__r__<sub>1</sub> = \(_x_<sub>1</sub>, _y_<sub>1</sub>, _z_<sub>1</sub>\), __r__<sub>2</sub> = \(_x_<sub>2</sub>, _y_<sub>2</sub>, _z_<sub>2</sub>\) and so on}@}. In {@{[three-dimensional space](three-dimensional%20space.md)}@}, {@{each position vector}@} requires {@{three [coordinates](coordinate%20vector.md) to uniquely define the location of a point}@}, so there are {@{3<!-- markdown separator -->_N_ coordinates to uniquely define the configuration of the system}@}. These are all {@{specific points in space to locate the particles}@}; {@{a general point in space}@} is written {@{__r__ = \(_x_, _y_, _z_\)}@}. {@{The [velocity](velocity.md) of each particle}@} is {@{how fast the particle moves along its path of motion}@}, and is {@{the [time derivative](time%20derivative.md) of its position}@}, thus {@{$$\mathbf {v} _{1}={\frac {d\mathbf {r} _{1} }{dt} },\mathbf {v} _{2}={\frac {d\mathbf {r} _{2} }{dt} },\ldots ,\mathbf {v} _{N}={\frac {d\mathbf {r} _{N} }{dt} }.$$}@} In {@{Newtonian mechanics}@}, {@{the [equations of motion](equations%20of%20motion.md)}@} are given by {@{[Newton's laws](Newton's%20laws.md)}@}. {@{The second law "net [force](force.md) equals mass times [acceleration](acceleration.md)"}@}, {@{$$\sum \mathbf {F} =m{\frac {d^{2}\mathbf {r} }{dt^{2} } },$$}@} {@{applies to each particle}@}. For {@{an _N_-particle system in 3 dimensions}@}, there are {@{3<!-- markdown separator -->_N_ second-degree [ordinary differential equations](ordinary%20differential%20equation.md)}@} in {@{the positions of the particles to solve for}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:41.728Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:41.728Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:04.106Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:04.106Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:51.470Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:51.470Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:23.381Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:23.381Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:46.665Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:46.665Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:15.843Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:15.843Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:48:36.600Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:48:36.600Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:48:41.669Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:48:41.669Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:38.060Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:38.060Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:43:49.649Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:43:49.649Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:45.407Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:45.407Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:01:51.735Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:01:51.735Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:50:01.767Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:50:01.767Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:10:07.989Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:10:07.989Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:32:49.732Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:32:49.732Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:09.308Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:09.308Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:14.467Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:14.467Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:12.913Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:12.913Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:09:21.753Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:09:21.753Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:01.693Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:01.693Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:03.711Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:03.711Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:34.731Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:34.731Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:16.741Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:16.741Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:47.785Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:47.785Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:46.317Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:46.317Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:20:58.634Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:20:58.634Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:57.181Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:57.181Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:38.086Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:38.086Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:42.763Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:42.763Z-->

### Lagrangian

Instead of {@{forces}@}, {@{Lagrangian mechanics}@} uses {@{the [energies](energy.md) in the system}@}. {@{The central quantity of Lagrangian mechanics}@} is {@{the __Lagrangian__, a function which summarizes the dynamics of the entire system}@}. Overall, {@{the Lagrangian}@} has {@{units of energy}@}, but no {@{single expression for all physical systems}@}. Any function which {@{generates the correct equations of motion, in agreement with physical laws}@}, can be {@{taken as a Lagrangian}@}. It is nevertheless {@{possible to construct general expressions for large classes of applications}@}. {@{The _non-relativistic_ Lagrangian}@} for {@{a system of particles in the absence of an electromagnetic field}@} is given by<sup>[\[8\]](#^ref-8)</sup> {@{$$L=T-V,$$}@} where {@{$$T={\frac {1}{2} }\sum _{k=1}^{N}m_{k}v_{k}^{2}$$ is the total [kinetic energy](kinetic%20energy.md) of the system}@}, equaling {@{the [sum](summation.md) Σ of the kinetic energies of the $N$ particles}@}. {@{Each particle labeled $k$}@} has {@{mass $m_{k}$, and _v_<sub>_k_</sub><sup>2</sup> = __v__<sub>_k_</sub> · __v__<sub>_k_</sub> is the magnitude squared of its velocity}@}, equivalent to {@{the [dot product](dot%20product.md) of the velocity with itself}@}.<sup>[\[9\]](#^ref-9)</sup> <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:00.799Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:00.799Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:31:27.601Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:31:27.601Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:01.029Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:01.029Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:42:17.584Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:42:17.584Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:20:32.291Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:20:32.291Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:12.023Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:12.023Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:48.966Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:48.966Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:18.915Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:18.915Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:31.473Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:31.473Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:15:29.370Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:15:29.370Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:42:03.568Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:42:03.568Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:43.121Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:43.121Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:57.721Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:57.721Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:26.856Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:26.856Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:26.578Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:26.578Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:04.910Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:04.910Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:23.737Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:23.737Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:48:36.941Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:48:36.941Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:11.063Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:11.063Z-->

{@{Kinetic energy _T_}@} is {@{the energy of the system's motion}@} and is a function {@{only of the velocities __v__<sub>_k_</sub>, not the positions __r__<sub>_k_</sub>, nor time _t_}@}, so {@{_T_ = _T_\(__v__<sub>1</sub>, __v__<sub>2</sub>, ...\)}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:12.731Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:12.731Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:28:11.873Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:28:11.873Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:50.683Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:50.683Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:14:08.692Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:14:08.692Z-->

{@{_V_, the [potential energy](potential%20energy.md) of the system}@}, reflects {@{the energy of interaction between the particles}@}, i.e. {@{how much energy any one particle has due to all the others}@}, together with {@{any external influences}@}. For {@{[conservative forces](conservative%20force.md) \(e.g. [Newtonian gravity](Newtonian%20gravity.md)\)}@}, it is {@{a function of the position vectors of the particles only}@}, so {@{_V_ = _V_\(__r__<sub>1</sub>, __r__<sub>2</sub>, ...\)}@}. For those {@{non-conservative forces which can be derived from an appropriate potential \(e.g. [electromagnetic potential](electromagnetic%20potential.md)\)}@}, {@{the velocities will appear also}@}, {@{_V_ = _V_\(__r__<sub>1</sub>, __r__<sub>2</sub>, ..., __v__<sub>1</sub>, __v__<sub>2</sub>, ...\)}@}. If there is {@{some external field or external driving force changing with time}@}, {@{the potential changes with time}@}, so most generally {@{_V_ = _V_\(__r__<sub>1</sub>, __r__<sub>2</sub>, ..., __v__<sub>1</sub>, __v__<sub>2</sub>, ..., _t_\)}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:38.914Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:38.914Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:29:53.711Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:29:53.711Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:49.712Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:49.712Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:21.326Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:21.326Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:50:13.374Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:50:13.374Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:51.432Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:51.432Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:42.853Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:42.853Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:38:06.285Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:38:06.285Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:27:02.384Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:27:02.384Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:44.041Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:44.041Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T04:29:53.104Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T04:29:53.104Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:12.192Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:12.192Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:17:43.876Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:17:43.876Z-->

### mathematical formulation \(for finite particle systems\)

{@{An equivalent but more mathematically formal definition of the Lagrangian}@} is as follows.<sup>[\[10\]](#^ref-10)</sup> For {@{a system of _N_ particles in three-dimensional space}@}, {@{the [configuration space](configuration%20space%20(physics).md) of the system}@} is {@{a smooth manifold $Q\subseteq \mathbb {R} ^{3N}$}@}, where {@{each configuration $q\in Q$}@} specifies {@{the spatial positions of each of the particles at a given instant of time}@}, and {@{the manifold}@} is composed of {@{all the configurations that are allowed by the constraints on the system}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:56:44.218Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:56:44.218Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:17.099Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:17.099Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:28:49.882Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:28:49.882Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:21:32.199Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:21:32.199Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:34.740Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:34.740Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:08:33.281Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:08:33.281Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:37:06.641Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:37:06.641Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:47:24.187Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:47:24.187Z-->

{@{The Lagrangian}@} is {@{a smooth function}@}: {@{$L:TQ\times \mathbb {R} \to \mathbb {R}$}@}, where {@{$TQ\subseteq \mathbb {R} ^{6N}$ is the [tangent bundle](tangent%20bundle.md) of the configuration space}@}. That is, {@{each element in $TQ$}@} represents {@{both the positions and the velocities of the particles}@}, and can be written as {@{a tuple $(q_{1},\ldots ,q_{N},{\dot {q} }_{1},\ldots {\dot {q} }_{N})$ with $q_{i}$ and ${\dot {q} }_{i}$}@} specifying {@{a position and a velocity of the i'th particle respectively}@}. (annotation: {@{Each point in the manifold}@} has {@{a tangent space}@}, so {@{a point in that point's tangent space}@} can represent {@{the velocities at the system configuration represented by that point in the manifold}@}, and has {@{$3n$ dimensions}@}. {@{The tangent bundle}@} collects {@{the tangent spaces for all points in the manifold}@}, so can be indexed by {@{the system configuration}@}, hence {@{adding $3n$ more dimensions}@}.) {@{The time dependence $t\in \mathbb {R}$}@} allows for {@{the Lagrangian to describe time-dependent forces or potentials}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:21:14.864Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:21:14.864Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:36:54.800Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:36:54.800Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:51.891Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:51.891Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:09.840Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:09.840Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:50.692Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:50.692Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:38:58.986Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:38:58.986Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:33.524Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:33.524Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:14.563Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:14.563Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:07.519Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:07.519Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:14.382Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:14.382Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:52:54.463Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:52:54.463Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:49.006Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:49.006Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:34.239Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:34.239Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:51.692Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:51.692Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:45:47.540Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:45:47.540Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:11.331Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:11.331Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:02.080Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:02.080Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:35.191Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:35.191Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:03.246Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:03.246Z-->

{@{A [trajectory](trajectory.md) of the system}@} is {@{a smooth function $q:[t_{0},t_{1}]\to Q$}@}, describing {@{the evolution of the configuration over time}@}. {@{Its velocity}@} is {@{the time derivative ${\dot {q} }(t)\in T_{q(t)}Q$ of $q(t)$}@}, and {@{the pair $(q(t),{\dot {q} }(t))\in TQ$}@} is thus {@{an element of the bundle for any $t$}@}. {@{The [action functional](action%20functional.md) of the trajectory}@} can therefore be defined as {@{the integral of the Lagrangian along the path}@}: {@{$S[q]=\int _{t_{0} }^{t_{1} }L(q(t),{\dot {q} }(t),t)\,dt$}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:40:19.973Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:40:19.973Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:05.607Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:05.607Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:43.073Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:43.073Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:01:56.104Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:01:56.104Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:02.655Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:02.655Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:14:22.819Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:14:22.819Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:12.424Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:12.424Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:28:36.221Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:28:36.221Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:29:16.951Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:29:16.951Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:43:50.931Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:43:50.931Z-->

{@{The laws of motion in Lagrangian mechanics}@} are derived from {@{the postulate that among all trajectories between two given configurations}@}, {@{the actual one that will be taken by the system}@} must be {@{a critical point \(often but not necessarily a local minimum\) of the action functional}@}. This leads to {@{the [Euler–Lagrange equations](Euler–Lagrange%20equation.md)}@} \(see also below\). <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:01:59.471Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:01:59.471Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:54.773Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:54.773Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:14.278Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:14.278Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:55.960Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:55.960Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:20:57.823Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:20:57.823Z-->

## equations of motion

For {@{a system of particles with masses $m_{1},\ldots ,m_{N}$}@}, {@{the [kinetic energy](kinetic%20energy.md)}@} is: {@{$T(q,{\dot {q} })={\frac {1}{2} }\sum _{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\|{\dot {q} }_{i}\|^{2}$}@}, where {@{${\dot {q} }_{i}\in \mathbb {R} ^{3}$ is the velocity of particle _i_}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:22.873Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:22.873Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:17:35.451Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:17:35.451Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:30.399Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:30.399Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:21.028Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:21.028Z-->

{@{The [potential energy](potential%20energy.md) $V(q,t)$}@} depends {@{only on the configuration \(and possibly on time\)}@}, and typically {@{arises from conservative forces}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:47:06.657Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:47:06.657Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:35.590Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:35.590Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:13.900Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:13.900Z-->

{@{The standard _Lagrangian_}@} is given by {@{the difference}@}: {@{$L(q,{\dot {q} },t)=T(q,{\dot {q} })-V(q,t)$}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:31:23.376Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:31:23.376Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:08:12.069Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:08:12.069Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:30:33.070Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:30:33.070Z-->

{@{This formulation}@} covers both {@{conservative and time-dependent systems}@} and forms the basis for {@{generalizations to continuous systems \(fields\), constrained systems, and systems with curved configuration spaces}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:20:25.461Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:20:25.461Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:54:07.824Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:54:07.824Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:48.819Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:48.819Z-->

If {@{_T_ or _V_ or both}@} depend {@{explicitly on time due to time-varying constraints or external influences}@}, {@{the Lagrangian _L_\(__r__<sub>1</sub>, __r__<sub>2</sub>, ... __v__<sub>1</sub>, __v__<sub>2</sub>, ... _t_\)}@} is {@{_explicitly time-dependent_}@}. If {@{neither the potential nor the kinetic energy depend on time}@}, then {@{the Lagrangian _L_\(__r__<sub>1</sub>, __r__<sub>2</sub>, ... __v__<sub>1</sub>, __v__<sub>2</sub>, ...\)}@} is {@{_explicitly independent of time_}@}. In {@{either case}@}, {@{the Lagrangian}@} always has {@{implicit time dependence through the generalized coordinates}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:02.808Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:02.808Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:21.818Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:21.818Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:35.201Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:35.201Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:43.011Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:43.011Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:50.639Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:50.639Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:00.357Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:00.357Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:42:27.806Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:42:27.806Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:39:55.891Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:39:55.891Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:54:06.942Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:54:06.942Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:02.587Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:02.587Z-->

With {@{these definitions}@}, {@{_Lagrange's equations_}@} are<sup>[\[11\]](#^ref-11)</sup> (annotation: {@{the longer equation}@} keeping {@{the constraint forces in the form of Lagrange multipliers}@}) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:46.119Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:46.119Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:00.724Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:00.724Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:46.187Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:46.187Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:00.796Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:00.796Z-->

|                                                                                                                                                                                        {@{__Lagrange's equations__}@}                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| :-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------: |
| {@{$${\frac {\partial L}{\partial \mathbf {r} _{k} } }-{\frac {\mathrm {d} }{\mathrm {d} t} }{\frac {\partial L}{\partial {\dot {\mathbf {r} } }_{k} } }+\sum _{i=1}^{C}\lambda _{i}{\frac {\partial f_{i} }{\partial \mathbf {r} _{k} } }=0,$$}@} (annotation: mnemonics: {@{force - change in momentum + constraint force = 0}@}; compare with {@{Newton's second law}@}: {@{force = change in momentum}@}) | <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:47.233Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:47.233Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:37.495Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:37.495Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:56.494Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:56.494Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:57.021Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:57.021Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:35.772Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:35.772Z-->

where {@{_k_ = 1, 2, ..., _N_ labels the particles}@}, there is {@{a [Lagrange multiplier](Lagrange%20multiplier.md) _λ<sub>i</sub>_ for each constraint equation _f_<sub>_i_</sub>}@}, and {@{$${\frac {\partial }{\partial \mathbf {r} _{k} } }\equiv \left({\frac {\partial }{\partial x_{k} } },{\frac {\partial }{\partial y_{k} } },{\frac {\partial }{\partial z_{k} } }\right),\quad {\frac {\partial }{\partial {\dot {\mathbf {r} } }_{k} } }\equiv \left({\frac {\partial }{\partial {\dot {x} }_{k} } },{\frac {\partial }{\partial {\dot {y} }_{k} } },{\frac {\partial }{\partial {\dot {z} }_{k} } }\right)$$}@} are each {@{shorthands for a vector of [partial derivatives](partial%20derivative.md) ∂/∂ with respect to the indicated variables}@} \(not {@{a derivative with respect to the entire vector}@}\).<sup>[\[nb 1\]](#^nb-1)</sup> {@{Each overdot}@} is {@{a shorthand for a [time derivative](time%20derivative.md)}@}. {@{This procedure}@} does {@{increase the number of equations to solve compared to Newton's laws}@}, from {@{3<!-- markdown separator -->_N_ to 3<!-- markdown separator -->_N_ + _C_}@}, because there are {@{3<!-- markdown separator -->_N_ coupled second-order differential equations in the position coordinates and multipliers}@}, plus {@{_C_ constraint equations}@}. However, when solved {@{alongside the position coordinates of the particles}@}, {@{the multipliers}@} can yield {@{information about the constraint forces}@}. {@{The coordinates}@} do not {@{need to be eliminated by solving the constraint equations}@}. (annotation: You directly solve {@{a coupled system of $3N$ second-order differential equations}@} for {@{particle trajectories $r_k(t)$}@} alongside {@{$C$ constraint equations for multiplier functions $\lambda_i(t)$}@}, directly yielding {@{both position paths and constraint forces (like tension or normal reaction)}@}. You do _not_ need to construct {@{coordinate transformations into $3N-C$ generalized coordinates}@}, nor {@{algebraically eliminate dependent variables using $f_i(r) = 0$}@}. Just like {@{optimizing via the method of Lagrange multipliers $\nabla f + \sum \lambda_i \nabla g = 0$}@}, you solve for {@{motion and constraint forces simultaneously in full Cartesian space}@}.) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:17.474Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:17.474Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:43:28.393Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:43:28.393Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:17.084Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:17.084Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:30.575Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:30.575Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:25:51.231Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:25:51.231Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:12:16.619Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:12:16.619Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:10.704Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:10.704Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:39:54.328Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:39:54.328Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:30:32.344Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:30:32.344Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:10:25.626Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:10:25.626Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:49:58.563Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:49:58.563Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:27:50.797Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:27:50.797Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:31:29.651Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:31:29.651Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:08:30.863Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:08:30.863Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:50.995Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:50.995Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:01:52.689Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:01:52.689Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:31.498Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:31.498Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:39.773Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:39.773Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:33:53.453Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:33:53.453Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:43.049Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:43.049Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:03.649Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:03.649Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:42:32.931Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:42:32.931Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:29:35.833Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:29:35.833Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:47.620Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:47.620Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:54.643Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:54.643Z-->

In {@{the Lagrangian}@}, {@{the position coordinates and velocity components}@} are {@{all [independent variables](dependent%20and%20independent%20variables.md#in%20pure%20mathematics)}@}, and {@{derivatives of the Lagrangian}@} are taken with respect to {@{these separately according to the usual [differentiation rules](differentiation%20rules.md)}@} \(e.g. {@{the partial derivative}@} of _L_ with respect to {@{the _z_ velocity component of particle 2}@}, defined by {@{_v_<sub>_z_,2</sub> = _dz_<sub>2</sub>/<!-- markdown separator -->_dt_}@}, is {@{just ∂<!-- markdown separator -->_L_<!-- markdown separator -->/∂<!-- markdown separator -->_v_<sub>_z_,2</sub>}@}; no {@{awkward [chain rules](chain%20rule.md) or total derivatives}@} need to be used to relate {@{the velocity component to the corresponding coordinate _z_<sub>2</sub>}@}\). <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:43.688Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:43.688Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:21:26.970Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:21:26.970Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:25.629Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:25.629Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:47.480Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:47.480Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:42:39.489Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:42:39.489Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:20.372Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:20.372Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:13.229Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:13.229Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:09.661Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:09.661Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:41.570Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:41.570Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:30:12.264Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:30:12.264Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:13.655Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:13.655Z-->

In {@{each constraint equation}@}, {@{one coordinate is redundant}@} because it is {@{determined from the other coordinates}@}. {@{The number of _independent_ coordinates}@} is therefore {@{_n_ = 3<!-- markdown separator -->_N_ − _C_}@}. We can transform {@{each position vector to a common set of _n_ [generalized coordinates](generalized%20coordinates.md)}@}, conveniently written as {@{an _n_-tuple __q__ = \(_q_<sub>1</sub>, _q_<sub>2</sub>, ... _q<sub>n</sub>_\)}@}, by expressing {@{each position vector, and hence the position coordinates}@}, as {@{[functions](function%20(mathematics).md) of the generalized coordinates and time}@}: {@{$$\mathbf {r} _{k}=\mathbf {r} _{k}(\mathbf {q} ,t)={\big (}x_{k}(\mathbf {q} ,t),y_{k}(\mathbf {q} ,t),z_{k}(\mathbf {q} ,t),t{\big )}.$$}@} {@{The vector __q__}@} is {@{a point in the [configuration space](configuration%20space%20(physics).md) of the system}@}. {@{The time derivatives of the generalized coordinates}@} are called {@{the generalized velocities}@}, and for each {@{particle the transformation of its velocity vector}@}, {@{the [total derivative](total%20derivative.md#total%20derivative) of its position with respect to time}@}, is {@{$${\dot {q} }_{j}={\frac {\mathrm {d} q_{j} }{\mathrm {d} t} },\quad \mathbf {v} _{k}=\sum _{j=1}^{n}{\frac {\partial \mathbf {r} _{k} }{\partial q_{j} } }{\dot {q} }_{j}+{\frac {\partial \mathbf {r} _{k} }{\partial t} }.$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:15.004Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:15.004Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:58.261Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:58.261Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:21:13.967Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:21:13.967Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:09:17.553Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:09:17.553Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:58.912Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:58.912Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:43:26.718Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:43:26.718Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:06:57.223Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:06:57.223Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:19.202Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:19.202Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:08.717Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:08.717Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:11.246Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:11.246Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:15.713Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:15.713Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:25.095Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:25.095Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:17:45.170Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:17:45.170Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:45:46.325Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:45:46.325Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:40:42.556Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:40:42.556Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:32:24.989Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:32:24.989Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:17.927Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:17.927Z-->

Given {@{this __v__<sub>_k_</sub>}@}, {@{the kinetic energy _in generalized coordinates_}@} depends on {@{the generalized velocities, generalized coordinates}@}, and {@{time if the position vectors depend explicitly on time due to time-varying constraints}@}, so {@{$T=T(\mathbf {q} ,{\dot {\mathbf {q} } },t)$}@}. (annotation: Compare to the case in {@{Cartesian coordinates}@} where {@{the kinetic energy _T_ depends only on the velocities and not the positions or time}@}.) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:42.794Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:42.794Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:28:12.619Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:28:12.619Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:28.148Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:28.148Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:19.790Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:19.790Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:09:27.830Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:09:27.830Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:22.552Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:22.552Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:30.100Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:30.100Z-->

With {@{these definitions}@}, {@{the __[Euler–Lagrange equations](Euler–Lagrange%20equation.md)__}@},<sup>[\[12\]](#^ref-12)</sup><sup>[\[13\]](#^ref-13)</sup> (annotation: {@{the shorter equation}@} with {@{the constraint force term eliminated}@}) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:40:18.571Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:40:18.571Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:28.829Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:28.829Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:18.451Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:18.451Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:56.261Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:56.261Z-->

|                                                                                                                                                                                                          {@{__Lagrange's equations__ (annotation: __Euler–Lagrange equations__)}@}                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------: |
| {@{$${\frac {\mathrm {d} }{\mathrm {d} t} }\left({\frac {\partial L}{\partial {\dot {q} }_{j} } }\right)={\frac {\partial L}{\partial q_{j} } }$$}@} (annotation: mnemonics: {@{change in generalized momentum = generalized force}@}; compare with the previous Lagrange's equations: {@{generalized force - change in generalized momentum + constraint force = 0}@}, we can see that {@{the constraint force term has been eliminated}@} due to {@{the use of generalized coordinates}@}) | <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:26.953Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:26.953Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:10:46.913Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:10:46.913Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:40:40.218Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:40:40.218Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:41:55.861Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:41:55.861Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:35.246Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:35.246Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:07.587Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:07.587Z-->

(annotation: {@{The Euler–Lagrange equations}@}) are {@{mathematical results from the [calculus of variations](calculus%20of%20variations.md)}@}, which can also be used in {@{mechanics}@}. Substituting in {@{the Lagrangian _L_\(__q__, d<!-- markdown separator -->__q__<!-- markdown separator -->/d<!-- markdown separator -->_t_}@}, _t_\) gives {@{the [equations of motion](equations%20of%20motion.md) of the system}@}. {@{The number of equations}@} has {@{decreased compared to Newtonian mechanics}@}, from {@{3<!-- markdown separator -->_N_ to _n_ = 3<!-- markdown separator -->_N_ − _C_ coupled second-order differential equations}@} in {@{the generalized coordinates}@}. {@{These equations}@} do not {@{include constraint forces at all}@}, only {@{non-constraint forces need to be accounted for}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:53.526Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:53.526Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:55:54.684Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:55:54.684Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:15.983Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:15.983Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:31:26.405Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:31:26.405Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:42:52.325Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:42:52.325Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:21.644Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:21.644Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:31:21.336Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:31:21.336Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:21:16.063Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:21:16.063Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:15:32.391Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:15:32.391Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:09:06.221Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:09:06.221Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:34:30.027Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:34:30.027Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:40.647Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:40.647Z-->

Although {@{the equations of motion include [partial derivatives](partial%20derivative.md)}@}, {@{the results of the partial derivatives}@} are still {@{[ordinary differential equations](ordinary%20differential%20equation.md) in the position coordinates of the particles}@}. {@{The [total time derivative](total%20derivative.md#total%20derivative) denoted d/d<!-- markdown separator -->_t_}@} often involves {@{[implicit differentiation](implicit%20differentiation.md)}@}. {@{Both equations}@} are {@{linear in the Lagrangian}@}, but generally are {@{nonlinear coupled equations in the coordinates}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:06:51.144Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:06:51.144Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:36.660Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:36.660Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:30.587Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:30.587Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:31:24.126Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:31:24.126Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:43:47.588Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:43:47.588Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:28:18.885Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:28:18.885Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:43:30.685Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:43:30.685Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:48.460Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:48.460Z-->

### extensions

As {@{already noted}@}, {@{this form of _L_}@} is applicable to {@{many important classes of system, but not everywhere}@}. For {@{[relativistic Lagrangian mechanics](relativistic%20Lagrangian%20mechanics.md)}@} it must be {@{replaced as a whole by a function consistent with special relativity \(scalar under Lorentz transformations\)}@} or {@{general relativity \(4-scalar\)}@}.<sup>[\[14\]](#^ref-14)</sup> Where {@{a magnetic field is present}@}, {@{the expression for the potential energy needs restating}@}.<sup>\[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation%20needed)_\]</sup> And for {@{dissipative forces \(e.g., [friction](friction.md)\)}@}, {@{another function must be introduced alongside Lagrangian}@} often referred to as a {@{"[Rayleigh dissipation function](Rayleigh%20dissipation%20function.md)" to account for the loss of energy}@}.<sup>[\[15\]](#^ref-15)</sup> <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:43:31.610Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:43:31.610Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:48.160Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:48.160Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:57:24.486Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:57:24.486Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:39.394Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:39.394Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:20.263Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:20.263Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:42:22.419Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:42:22.419Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:17.322Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:17.322Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:42:26.898Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:42:26.898Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:25:53.133Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:25:53.133Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:28:01.569Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:28:01.569Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:24.191Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:24.191Z-->

{@{One or more of the particles}@} may each be subject to {@{one or more [holonomic constraints](holonomic%20constraints.md)}@}; {@{such a constraint}@} is described by {@{an equation of the form _f_\(__r__, _t_\) = 0}@}. If {@{the number of constraints in the system is _C_}@}, then {@{each constraint}@} has {@{an equation _f_<sub>1</sub>\(__r__, _t_\) = 0, _f_<sub>2</sub>\(__r__, _t_\) = 0, ..., _f<sub>C</sub>_\(__r__, _t_\) = 0}@}, each of which could {@{apply to any of the particles}@}. If {@{particle _k_ is subject to constraint _i_}@}, then {@{_f<sub>i</sub>_\(__r__<sub>_k_</sub>, _t_\) = 0}@}. At {@{any instant of time}@}, {@{the coordinates of a constrained particle}@} are {@{linked together and not independent}@}. {@{The constraint equations}@} determine {@{the allowed paths the particles can move along}@}, but not {@{where they are or how fast they go at every instant of time}@}. {@{[Nonholonomic constraints](nonholonomic%20constraints.md)}@} depend on {@{the particle velocities, accelerations, or higher derivatives of position}@}. {@{Lagrangian mechanics}@} _can only {@{be applied to systems whose constraints, if any, are all holonomic}@}_. {@{Three examples of nonholonomic constraints}@} are:<sup>[\[16\]](#^ref-16)</sup> when {@{the constraint equations are non-integrable}@}, when {@{the constraints have inequalities}@}, or when {@{the constraints involve complicated non-conservative forces like friction}@}. {@{Nonholonomic constraints}@} require {@{special treatment}@}, and one may have to {@{revert to [Newtonian mechanics](Newton's%20laws%20of%20motion.md) or use other methods}@}.<sup>[\[17\]](#^ref-17)</sup> <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:19.218Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:19.218Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:08:38.003Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:08:38.003Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:28:34.783Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:28:34.783Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:39.380Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:39.380Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:55.353Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:55.353Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:38.147Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:38.147Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:37:13.886Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:37:13.886Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:29.113Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:29.113Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:30:57.350Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:30:57.350Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:43:35.158Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:43:35.158Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:08:35.870Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:08:35.870Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:40.595Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:40.595Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:13.502Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:13.502Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:28:06.553Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:28:06.553Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:09.603Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:09.603Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:08:52.505Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:08:52.505Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:47:26.065Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:47:26.065Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:57.842Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:57.842Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:36:05.405Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:36:05.405Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:22.311Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:22.311Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:42:01.587Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:42:01.587Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:21:22.527Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:21:22.527Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:38.552Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:38.552Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:35.412Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:35.412Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:12:17.552Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:12:17.552Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:17:29.987Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:17:29.987Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:49.796Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:49.796Z-->

## from Newtonian to Lagrangian mechanics

### Newton's laws

> {@{![Portrait of [Isaac Newton](//commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton) \(1642-1727\). This is a copy of a painting by Sir Godfrey Kneller \(1689\).](../../archives/Wikimedia%20Commons/GodfreyKneller-IsaacNewton-1689.jpg)}@}
>
> {@{[Isaac Newton](Isaac%20Newton.md) \(1642–1727\)}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:48.542Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:48.542Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:56.627Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:56.627Z-->

For {@{simplicity}@}, {@{Newton's laws}@} can be {@{illustrated for one particle without much loss of generality}@} \(for {@{a system of _N_ particles}@}, {@{all of these equations}@} apply to {@{each particle in the system}@}\). {@{The [equation of motion](equation%20of%20motion.md) for a particle of constant mass _m_}@} is {@{[Newton's second law](Newton's%20second%20law.md#second%20law) of 1687}@}, in {@{modern vector notation $$\mathbf {F} =m\mathbf {a} ,$$}@} where {@{__a__ is its acceleration and __F__ the resultant force acting _on_ it}@}. Where {@{the mass is varying}@}, the equation needs to be {@{generalised to take the time derivative of the momentum}@}. In {@{three spatial dimensions}@}, this is {@{a system of three coupled second-order [ordinary differential equations](ordinary%20differential%20equation.md) to solve}@}, since there are {@{three components in this vector equation}@}. {@{The solution}@} is {@{the position vector __r__ of the particle at time _t_}@}, subject to {@{the [initial conditions](initial%20condition.md) of __r__ and __v__ when _t_ = 0}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:34:51.137Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:34:51.137Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:13.453Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:13.453Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:17.042Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:17.042Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:50.923Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:50.923Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:49.330Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:49.330Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:57:20.973Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:57:20.973Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:04:01.926Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:04:01.926Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:41.429Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:41.429Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:28:10.676Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:28:10.676Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:23.567Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:23.567Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:21:28.371Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:21:28.371Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:07.526Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:07.526Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:31:28.518Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:31:28.518Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:25.971Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:25.971Z!fsrs,2026-08-21T06:57:11.421Z,4,3.94605407,1,2,2,0,0,2026-08-17T06:57:11.421Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:28:44.630Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:28:44.630Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:20.799Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:20.799Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:29.338Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:29.338Z-->

{@{Newton's laws}@} are {@{easy to use in Cartesian coordinates}@}, but {@{Cartesian coordinates are not always convenient}@}, and for {@{other coordinate systems the equations of motion can become complicated}@}. In {@{a set of [curvilinear coordinates](curvilinear%20coordinates.md) ___ξ___ = \(_ξ_<sup>1</sup>, _ξ_<sup>2</sup>, _ξ_<sup>3</sup>\)}@}, {@{the law in [tensor index notation](tensor%20index%20notation.md)}@} is {@{the _"Lagrangian form"_}@}<sup>[\[18\]](#^ref-18)</sup><sup>[\[19\]](#^ref-19)</sup> {@{$$F^{a}=m\left({\frac {\mathrm {d} ^{2}\xi ^{a} }{\mathrm {d} t^{2} } }+\Gamma ^{a}{}_{bc}{\frac {\mathrm {d} \xi ^{b} }{\mathrm {d} t} }{\frac {\mathrm {d} \xi ^{c} }{\mathrm {d} t} }\right)=g^{ak}\left({\frac {\mathrm {d} }{\mathrm {d} t} }{\frac {\partial T}{\partial {\dot {\xi } }^{k} } }-{\frac {\partial T}{\partial \xi ^{k} } }\right),\quad {\dot {\xi } }^{a}\equiv {\frac {\mathrm {d} \xi ^{a} }{\mathrm {d} t} },$$}@} where _F_<sup>_a_</sup> is {@{the _a_-th [contravariant component](covariance%20and%20contravariance%20of%20vectors.md) of the resultant force acting on the particle}@}, Γ<sup>_a_</sup><sub>_bc_</sub> are {@{the [Christoffel symbols](Christoffel%20symbols.md) of the second kind}@}, {@{$$T={\frac {1}{2} }mg_{bc}{\frac {\mathrm {d} \xi ^{b} }{\mathrm {d} t} }{\frac {\mathrm {d} \xi ^{c} }{\mathrm {d} t} }$$}@} is {@{the kinetic energy of the particle}@}, and _g<sub>bc</sub>_ {@{the [covariant components](covariance%20and%20contravariance%20of%20vectors.md) of the _[metric tensor](metric%20tensor.md)_ of the curvilinear coordinate system}@}. {@{All the indices _a_, _b_, _c_}@}, each take {@{the values 1, 2, 3}@}. {@{Curvilinear coordinates}@} are not {@{the same as generalized coordinates}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:33.257Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:33.257Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:01:36.627Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:01:36.627Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:50:17.424Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:50:17.424Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:08.546Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:08.546Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:30:34.707Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:30:34.707Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:02.092Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:02.092Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:23.860Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:23.860Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:56:55.083Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:56:55.083Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:26:42.941Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:26:42.941Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:33:47.348Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:33:47.348Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:50:14.867Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:50:14.867Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:56.061Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:56.061Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:58.841Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:58.841Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:38:04.101Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:38:04.101Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:38.613Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:38.613Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:46.101Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:46.101Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:11.772Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:11.772Z-->

(annotation: To derive {@{the "Lagrangian form" of Newton's law}@}, consider {@{a path in curvilinear coordinates $\xi^a(t)$}@}. We will use {@{Ricci calculus notation here}@}. Note that time $t$ is {@{not part of the spatial coordinates}@}, so we use {@{the overdot notation for time derivatives instead of Ricci calculus notation}@}, which is {@{inapplicable to time in this context}@}. {@{Ordinary partial differentiation}@} fails to yield {@{physical variation because basis vectors change across space}@}. We first construct {@{the general spatial covariant derivative}@} {@{$\nabla_b \dot{\xi}^a = \partial_b \dot{\xi}^a + \Gamma^a_{bc} \dot{\xi}^c$}@}, which captures both {@{component variation and the continuous rotation and scaling of the underlying coordinate basis}@}. Analogous to how {@{the standard directional derivative $u^b \partial_b f$}@} contracts {@{a spatial gradient with a velocity vector}@} to measure {@{rate of change along a path}@}, contracting {@{the spatial covariant derivative with the directional vector $\dot{\xi}^b$}@} yields {@{the intrinsic time derivative along the particle's trajectory}@}. For {@{physical acceleration}@}, applying {@{the multivariable chain rule to the coordinate velocity components along the path $\xi^b(t)$}@} gives {@{their naive coordinate rate of change}@} as {@{$\ddot{\xi}^a = \frac{d}{dt}\dot{\xi}^a = \frac{\partial \dot{\xi}^a}{\partial \xi^b}\frac{d\xi^b}{dt} = \dot{\xi}^b \partial_b \dot{\xi}^a$}@}. Replacing this {@{partial derivative with the spatial covariant derivative}@} yields {@{the true physical acceleration}@} {@{$\frac{D \dot{\xi}^a}{dt} = \dot{\xi}^b \nabla_b \dot{\xi}^a = \dot{\xi}^b(\partial_b \dot{\xi}^a + \Gamma^a_{bc} \dot{\xi}^c) = \ddot{\xi}^a + \Gamma^a_{bc}\dot{\xi}^b\dot{\xi}^c$}@}, where {@{the additional Christoffel term}@} directly accounts for {@{the coordinate basis rotating and stretching along the path of motion}@}.) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:34:35.877Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:34:35.877Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:00.237Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:00.237Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:27.735Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:27.735Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:27.530Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:27.530Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:44.062Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:44.062Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:37.705Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:37.705Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:27:47.362Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:27:47.362Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:53.016Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:53.016Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:11.474Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:11.474Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:36:02.081Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:36:02.081Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:54.472Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:54.472Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:19.458Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:19.458Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:40:35.734Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:40:35.734Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:09:25.645Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:09:25.645Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:36.582Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:36.582Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:18:12.417Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:18:12.417Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:50.631Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:50.631Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:14.817Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:14.817Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:28:11.665Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:28:11.665Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:54:55.785Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:54:55.785Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:52.189Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:52.189Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:10.224Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:10.224Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:32.304Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:32.304Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:38.950Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:38.950Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:28.293Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:28.293Z-->

(annotation: To prove that {@{Newton's second law in contravariant form, $m \frac{D\dot{\xi}^a}{dt} = F^a$}@}, is equivalent to {@{the kinetic energy formulation $g^{ab} \left[ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial \dot{\xi}^b}\right) - \frac{\partial T}{\partial \xi^b} \right] = F^a$}@}, define {@{$T = \frac{1}{2} m g_{cd} \dot{\xi}^c \dot{\xi}^d$}@}. Taking {@{the partial derivative with respect to coordinate velocity}@} yields {@{$\frac{\partial T}{\partial \dot{\xi}^b} = m g_{bc} \dot{\xi}^c$}@}. Taking its {@{total time derivative requires applying the product rule}@}, giving {@{$\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial \dot{\xi}^b}\right) = m \left( \frac{d}{dt}(g_{bc}) \dot{\xi}^c + g_{bc} \frac{d}{dt}(\dot{\xi}^c) \right)$}@}, where expanding {@{the metric's time derivative explicitly via the multivariable chain rule}@} gives {@{$\frac{d}{dt}(g_{bc}) = \frac{\partial g_{bc} }{\partial \xi^d} \frac{d\xi^d}{dt} = \partial_d g_{bc} \dot{\xi}^d$}@}, resulting in {@{$\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial \dot{\xi}^b}\right) = m g_{bc}\ddot{\xi}^c + m \partial_d g_{bc}\dot{\xi}^c\dot{\xi}^d$}@}. Subtracting {@{the spatial gradient $\frac{\partial T}{\partial \xi^b} = \frac{1}{2} m \partial_b g_{cd} \dot{\xi}^c \dot{\xi}^d$}@} and exploiting {@{the index symmetry of $\dot{\xi}^c\dot{\xi}^d$}@} to rewrite {@{$\partial_d g_{bc}\dot{\xi}^c\dot{\xi}^d$ as $\frac{1}{2}(\partial_d g_{bc} + \partial_c g_{bd})\dot{\xi}^c\dot{\xi}^d$}@} yields {@{$\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial \dot{\xi}^b}\right) - \frac{\partial T}{\partial \xi^b} = m g_{bc}\ddot{\xi}^c + \frac{1}{2} m (\partial_d g_{bc} + \partial_c g_{bd} - \partial_b g_{cd})\dot{\xi}^c\dot{\xi}^d$}@}. To derive {@{the metric combination in brackets}@}, define the metric as {@{the inner product of basis vectors $g_{cd} = e_c \cdot e_d$}@} and the Christoffel symbol via {@{basis vector differentiation $\partial_d e_c = \Gamma^e_{cd} e_e$}@}, making {@{the lowered Christoffel symbol $\Gamma_{b,cd} = g_{be} \Gamma^e_{cd} = e_b \cdot \partial_d e_c$}@}. {@{Differentiating the metric tensor}@} yields {@{$\partial_d g_{bc} = \partial_d(e_b \cdot e_c) = (\partial_d e_b) \cdot e_c + e_b \cdot (\partial_d e_c) = \Gamma_{c,bd} + \Gamma_{b,cd}$}@}. Forming {@{the symmetric combination $(\Gamma_{c,bd} + \Gamma_{b,cd}) + (\Gamma_{d,bc} + \Gamma_{b,cd}) - (\Gamma_{c,bd} + \Gamma_{d,bc}) = 2\Gamma_{b,cd}$}@} establishes {@{$\Gamma_{b,cd} = \frac{1}{2}(\partial_d g_{bc} + \partial_c g_{bd} - \partial_b g_{cd})$}@}. Substituting {@{this geometric identity simplifies the bracketed term}@} to {@{$m(g_{be}\ddot{\xi}^e + \Gamma_{b,cd}\dot{\xi}^c\dot{\xi}^d) = m g_{be}(\ddot{\xi}^e + \Gamma^e_{cd}\dot{\xi}^c\dot{\xi}^d) = m g_{be}\frac{D\dot{\xi}^e}{dt}$}@}. Finally, contracting {@{both sides with the inverse metric $g^{ab}$ raises the index}@}: {@{$g^{ab} \left[ \frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial \dot{\xi}^b}\right) - \frac{\partial T}{\partial \xi^b} \right] = g^{ab} m g_{be} \frac{D\dot{\xi}^e}{dt} = m \delta^a_e \frac{D\dot{\xi}^e}{dt} = m \frac{D\dot{\xi}^a}{dt} = F^a$}@}, proving {@{the contravariant equivalence}@}.) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:47.675Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:47.675Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:33:56.101Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:33:56.101Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:50:10.231Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:50:10.231Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:57:43.322Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:57:43.322Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:08.814Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:08.814Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:09:08.664Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:09:08.664Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:31:35.372Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:31:35.372Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:10:53.565Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:10:53.565Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:31:25.220Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:31:25.220Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:33:23.062Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:33:23.062Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:08.855Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:08.855Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:38:02.436Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:38:02.436Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:01.296Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:01.296Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:09.826Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:09.826Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:45.395Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:45.395Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T07:08:57.059Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T07:08:57.059Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:28:51.463Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:28:51.463Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:17.296Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:17.296Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:30.181Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:30.181Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:29:19.344Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:29:19.344Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:44.982Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:44.982Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:04:04.036Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:04:04.036Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:06.594Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:06.594Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:43:53.920Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:43:53.920Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:29.308Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:29.308Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:23.994Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:23.994Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:28:02.409Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:28:02.409Z-->

It may seem like an {@{overcomplication to cast Newton's law in this form}@}, but there are {@{advantages}@}. {@{The acceleration components in terms of the Christoffel symbols}@} can be {@{avoided by evaluating derivatives of the kinetic energy}@} instead. If there is no {@{resultant force acting on the particle, __F__ = __0__}@}, it does not {@{accelerate, but moves with constant velocity in a straight line}@}. Mathematically, {@{the solutions of the differential equation}@} are {@{_[geodesics](geodesic.md)_, the curves of extremal length between two points in space}@} \(these may end up being {@{minimal, that is the shortest paths, but not necessarily}@}\). In {@{flat 3D real space}@} the geodesics are {@{simply straight lines}@}. So for {@{a free particle}@}, {@{Newton's second law}@} coincides with {@{the geodesic equation}@} and states that {@{free particles follow geodesics, the extremal trajectories it can move along}@}. If the particle is {@{subject to forces __F__ ≠ __0__}@}, the particle {@{accelerates due to forces acting on it and deviates away from the geodesics it would follow if free}@}. With {@{appropriate extensions of the quantities given here}@} in flat 3D space to {@{4D [curved spacetime](curved%20spacetime.md)}@}, {@{the above form of Newton's law}@} also carries over to {@{[Einstein](Albert%20Einstein.md)'s [general relativity](general%20relativity.md)}@}, in which case {@{free particles follow geodesics in curved spacetime}@} that are no longer {@{"straight lines" in the ordinary sense}@}.<sup>[\[20\]](#^ref-20)</sup> <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:52.816Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:52.816Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:32.298Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:32.298Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:20:29.738Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:20:29.738Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:44.113Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:44.113Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:22.878Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:22.878Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:18.074Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:18.074Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:31:20.589Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:31:20.589Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:20:28.078Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:20:28.078Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:54.541Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:54.541Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:35.852Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:35.852Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:20.607Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:20.607Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:29.351Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:29.351Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:12.539Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:12.539Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T04:19:58.643Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T04:19:58.643Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:32:11.654Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:32:11.654Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:50:40.024Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:50:40.024Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:51.938Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:51.938Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:12.636Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:12.636Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:25:15.283Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:25:15.283Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:25:51.247Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:25:51.247Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:12.455Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:12.455Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:15.923Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:15.923Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:01:55.308Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:01:55.308Z-->

However, we still need to know {@{the total resultant force __F__ acting on the particle}@}, which in turn requires {@{the resultant non-constraint force __N__ plus the resultant constraint force __C__}@}, {@{$$\mathbf {F} =\mathbf {C} +\mathbf {N} .$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:18:09.639Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:18:09.639Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:41.030Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:41.030Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:43.630Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:43.630Z-->

{@{The constraint forces}@} can be {@{complicated}@}, since they {@{generally depend on time}@}. Also, if there are {@{constraints}@}, {@{the curvilinear coordinates}@} are not {@{independent but related by one or more constraint equations}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:19.557Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:19.557Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:21.260Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:21.260Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:15.063Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:15.063Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:39:55.180Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:39:55.180Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:23.518Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:23.518Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:55.181Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:55.181Z-->

{@{The constraint forces}@} can either be {@{eliminated from the equations of motion}@}, so only {@{the non-constraint forces remain}@}, or {@{included by including the constraint equations in the equations of motion}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:52.789Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:52.789Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:27:40.790Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:27:40.790Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:23.422Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:23.422Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:33:50.686Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:33:50.686Z-->

>
> {@{Constraint force __C__ and virtual displacement _δ_<!-- markdown separator -->__r__}@} for {@{a particle of mass _m_ confined to a curve}@}. {@{The resultant non-constraint force}@} is {@{__N__}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:54:08.901Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:54:08.901Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:06.084Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:06.084Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:54.353Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:54.353Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:29:36.987Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:29:36.987Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:31.743Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:31.743Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:20.577Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:20.577Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:22.749Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:22.749Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:40:18.286Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:40:18.286Z-->

{@{A fundamental result in [analytical mechanics](analytical%20mechanics.md)}@} is {@{[D'Alembert's principle](d'Alembert's%20principle.md)}@}, introduced in {@{1708 by [Jacques Bernoulli](Jacques%20Bernoulli.md) to understand [static equilibrium](static%20equilibrium.md#static)}@}, and developed by {@{[D'Alembert](d'Alembert.md) in 1743 to solve dynamical problems}@}.<sup>[\[21\]](#^ref-21)</sup> {@{The principle asserts}@} for {@{_N_ particles the virtual work, i.e. the work along a virtual displacement, $\delta \mathbf {r} _{k}$}@}, (annotation: {@{The virtual work}@} here is generalized to {@{dynamics via d'Alembert's principle}@} by treating {@{inertial forces ($-m_k a_k$) as reversed effective forces}@} to establish {@{dynamic equilibrium}@}) is {@{zero:<sup>[\[9\]](#^ref-9)</sup> $$\sum _{k=1}^{N}(\mathbf {N} _{k}+\mathbf {C} _{k}-m_{k}\mathbf {a} _{k})\cdot \delta \mathbf {r} _{k}=0.$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:43.593Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:43.593Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:48:17.216Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:48:17.216Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:40:37.953Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:40:37.953Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:44:23.530Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:44:23.530Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:43:29.541Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:43:29.541Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:02.663Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:02.663Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:40.292Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:40.292Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:02.632Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:02.632Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:37.106Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:37.106Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:28:23.894Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:28:23.894Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:06.307Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:06.307Z-->

{@{The _[virtual displacements](virtual%20displacement.md)_, $\delta \mathbf {r} _{k}$}@}, are by {@{definition infinitesimal changes in the configuration of the system}@} consistent with {@{the constraint forces acting on the system _at an instant of time_}@},<sup>[\[22\]](#^ref-22)</sup> i.e. in {@{such a way that the constraint forces maintain the constrained motion}@}. They are not {@{the same as the actual displacements in the system}@}, which are caused by {@{the resultant constraint and non-constraint forces}@} acting on {@{the particle to accelerate and move it}@}.<sup>[\[nb 2\]](#^nb-2)</sup> {@{[Virtual work](virtual%20work.md)}@} is {@{the work done along a virtual displacement for any force \(constraint or non-constraint\)}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:57:05.750Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:57:05.750Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:16.513Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:16.513Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:16.086Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:16.086Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:01:46.321Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:01:46.321Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:10:51.260Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:10:51.260Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:00.301Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:00.301Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:30:30.172Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:30:30.172Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:34.521Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:34.521Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:22.010Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:22.010Z-->

Since {@{the constraint forces}@} act {@{perpendicular to the motion of each particle in the system to maintain the constraints}@}, {@{the total virtual work by the constraint forces acting on the system}@} is {@{zero:<sup>[\[23\]](#^ref-23)</sup><sup>[\[nb 3\]](#^nb-3)</sup> $$\sum _{k=1}^{N}\mathbf {C} _{k}\cdot \delta \mathbf {r} _{k}=0,$$}@} so that {@{$$\sum _{k=1}^{N}(\mathbf {N} _{k}-m_{k}\mathbf {a} _{k})\cdot \delta \mathbf {r} _{k}=0.$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:50:39.135Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:50:39.135Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:54:41.890Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:54:41.890Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:10.475Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:10.475Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:08.252Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:08.252Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:53.964Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:53.964Z-->

Thus {@{D'Alembert's principle}@} allows us to {@{concentrate on only the applied non-constraint forces}@}, and exclude {@{the constraint forces in the equations of motion}@}.<sup>[\[23\]](#^ref-23)</sup><sup>[\[24\]](#^ref-24)</sup> {@{The form shown}@} is also {@{independent of the choice of coordinates}@}. However, it cannot be {@{readily used to set up the equations of motion in an arbitrary coordinate system}@} since {@{the displacements $\delta \mathbf {r} _{k}$ might be connected by a constraint equation}@}, which prevents us from {@{setting the _N_ individual summands to 0}@}. We will therefore seek {@{a system of mutually independent coordinates}@} for which {@{the total sum will be 0 if and only if the individual summands are 0}@}. (annotation: By embedding {@{constraints directly into these coordinates}@}, {@{the virtual variations $\delta q_j$}@} become {@{unconstrained and arbitrary}@}, forcing {@{each individual coefficient to vanish independently}@} for {@{the sum to hold under all variations}@}.) Setting {@{each of the summands to 0}@} will eventually give us {@{our separated equations of motion}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:48:18.153Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:48:18.153Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:05.310Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:05.310Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:40:15.752Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:40:15.752Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:53.619Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:53.619Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:05.603Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:05.603Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:09:19.123Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:09:19.123Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:18.578Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:18.578Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:25.228Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:25.228Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:32:46.831Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:32:46.831Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:11.056Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:11.056Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:25:52.206Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:25:52.206Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:09.238Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:09.238Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:08:28.243Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:08:28.243Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:48.641Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:48.641Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:17.332Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:17.332Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T04:20:02.323Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T04:20:02.323Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:40:46.507Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:40:46.507Z-->

### equations of motion from d'Alembert's principle

If there are {@{constraints on particle _k_}@}, then since {@{the coordinates of the position __r__<sub>_k_</sub> = \(_x_<sub>_k_</sub>, _y_<sub>_k_</sub>, _z_<sub>_k_</sub>\)}@} are {@{linked together by a constraint equation}@}, so are those of {@{the [virtual displacements](virtual%20displacement.md) _δ_<!-- markdown separator -->__r__<sub>_k_</sub> = \(_δx_<sub>_k_</sub>, _δy_<sub>_k_</sub>, _δz_<sub>_k_</sub>\)}@}. Since {@{the generalized coordinates are independent}@}, we can avoid {@{the complications with the _δ_<!-- markdown separator -->__r__<sub>_k_</sub>}@} by {@{converting to virtual displacements in the generalized coordinates}@}. These are related in {@{the same form as a [total differential](total%20differential.md#differentials%20in%20several%20variables)}@},<sup>[\[9\]](#^ref-9)</sup> {@{$$\delta \mathbf {r} _{k}=\sum _{j=1}^{n}{\frac {\partial \mathbf {r} _{k} }{\partial q_{j} } }\delta q_{j}.$$}@} (annotation: This is just {@{the change-of-basis formula}@}.)
