If there are {@{more particles, in accordance with the above results}@}, {@{the total kinetic energy}@} is {@{a sum over all the particle kinetic energies}@}, and {@{the potential}@} is {@{a function of all the coordinates}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:24.603Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:24.603Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:20.165Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:20.165Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:50:32.943Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:50:32.943Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:01.902Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:01.902Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:59.675Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:59.675Z-->

#### Cartesian coordinates

{@{The Lagrangian of the particle}@} can be written {@{$$L(x,y,z,{\dot {x} },{\dot {y} },{\dot {z} })={\frac {1}{2} }m({\dot {x} }^{2}+{\dot {y} }^{2}+{\dot {z} }^{2})-V(x,y,z).$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:27:57.890Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:27:57.890Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:13.723Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:13.723Z-->

(annotation: the Lagrangian: {@{$L = {\frac {1}{2} }m({\dot {x} }^{2}+{\dot {y} }^{2}+{\dot {z} }^{2})-V$}@}) {@{The equations of motion for the particle}@} are found by applying {@{the [Euler–Lagrange equation](Euler–Lagrange%20equation.md)}@}, for {@{the _x_ coordinate $${\frac {\mathrm {d} }{\mathrm {d} t} }\left({\frac {\partial L}{\partial {\dot {x} } } }\right)={\frac {\partial L}{\partial x} },$$}@} with derivatives {@{$${\frac {\partial L}{\partial x} }=-{\frac {\partial V}{\partial x} },\quad {\frac {\partial L}{\partial {\dot {x} } } }=m{\dot {x} },\quad {\frac {\mathrm {d} }{\mathrm {d} t} }\left({\frac {\partial L}{\partial {\dot {x} } } }\right)=m{\ddot {x} },$$}@} hence {@{$$m{\ddot {x} }=-{\frac {\partial V}{\partial x} },$$}@} and similarly for {@{the _y_ and _z_ coordinates}@}. {@{Collecting the equations in vector form}@} we find {@{$$m{\ddot {\mathbf {r} } }=-{\boldsymbol {\nabla } }V$$}@} which is {@{[Newton's second law of motion](Newton's%20second%20law%20of%20motion.md#Newton%27s%20second%20law) for a particle subject to a conservative force}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:39.681Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:39.681Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:24:16.990Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:24:16.990Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:32.555Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:32.555Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:09.244Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:09.244Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:37.081Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:37.081Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:27:37.097Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:27:37.097Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:15:31.485Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:15:31.485Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:57:00.347Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:57:00.347Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:20.105Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:20.105Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:21.186Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:21.186Z-->

#### polar coordinates in 2D and 3D

Using {@{the spherical coordinates \(_r_, _θ_, _φ_\)}@} as {@{commonly used in physics \(ISO 80000-2:2019 convention\)}@}, where {@{_r_ is the radial distance to origin, _θ_ is polar angle \(also known as colatitude, zenith angle, normal angle, or inclination angle\), and _φ_ is the azimuthal angle}@}, {@{the Lagrangian for a central potential}@} is {@{$$L={\frac {m}{2} }({\dot {r} }^{2}+r^{2}{\dot {\theta } }^{2}+r^{2}\sin ^{2}\theta \,{\dot {\varphi } }^{2})-V(r).$$}@} (annotation: Recall {@{the metric tensor for the spherical coordinates}@} is {@{$\operatorname {diag}(1, r^2, r^2 \sin^2 \theta)$}@}.) So, in {@{spherical coordinates}@}, {@{the Euler–Lagrange equations}@} are (annotation: for $r$) {@{$$m{\ddot {r} }-mr({\dot {\theta } }^{2}+\sin ^{2}\theta \,{\dot {\varphi } }^{2})+{\frac {\partial V}{\partial r} }=0,$$}@} <br/> (annotation: for $\theta$) {@{$${\frac {\mathrm {d} }{\mathrm {d} t} }(mr^{2}{\dot {\theta } })-mr^{2}\sin \theta \cos \theta \,{\dot {\varphi } }^{2}=0,$$}@} <br/> (annotation: for $\varphi$) {@{$${\frac {\mathrm {d} }{\mathrm {d} t} }(mr^{2}\sin ^{2}\theta \,{\dot {\varphi } })=0.$$}@} {@{The _φ_ coordinate is cyclic}@} since it {@{does not appear in the Lagrangian}@}, so {@{the conserved momentum in the system is the angular momentum}@} {@{$$p_{\varphi }={\frac {\partial L}{\partial {\dot {\varphi } } } }=mr^{2}\sin ^{2}\theta {\dot {\varphi } },$$}@} in which {@{_r_, _θ_ and _dφ_<!-- markdown separator -->/<!-- markdown separator -->_dt_ can all vary with time}@}, but only in {@{such a way that _p_<sub>_φ_</sub> is constant}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:09:10.992Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:09:10.992Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:49.471Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:49.471Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:08:26.186Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:08:26.186Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:43.994Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:43.994Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:47.494Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:47.494Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:51:46.110Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:51:46.110Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:08.581Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:08.581Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:21:12.143Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:21:12.143Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:45:45.243Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:45:45.243Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:43:40.147Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:43:40.147Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:14:06.094Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:14:06.094Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:16:36.998Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:16:36.998Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:29:39.749Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:29:39.749Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:40:52.861Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:40:52.861Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:40:45.515Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:40:45.515Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:25:48.074Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:25:48.074Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:20.718Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:20.718Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:18.330Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:18.330Z-->

{@{The Lagrangian in two-dimensional polar coordinates}@} is {@{recovered}@} by {@{fixing _θ_ to the constant value _π_<!-- markdown separator -->/2}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:52.271Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:52.271Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:34:50.522Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:34:50.522Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:42.340Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:42.340Z-->

### pendulum on a movable support

> {@{![File for description of physics problem on [Lagrangian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrangian).](../../archives/Wikimedia%20Commons/PendulumWithMovableSupport.svg)}@}
>
> {@{Sketch of the situation}@} with {@{definition of the coordinates}@} \(click to enlarge\) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:51.879Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:51.879Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:04:04.663Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:04:04.663Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:26.205Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:26.205Z-->

Consider {@{a pendulum of mass _m_ and length _ℓ_}@}, which is attached to {@{a support with mass _M_}@}, which can {@{move along a line in the $x$-direction}@}. Let $x$ be {@{the coordinate along the line of the support}@}, and let us denote {@{the position of the pendulum by the angle $\theta$ from the vertical}@}. (annotation: This chooses {@{the generalized coordinates}@}.) {@{The coordinates and velocity components}@} of the pendulum bob are {@{$${\begin{array}{rll}&x_{\mathrm {pend} }=x+\ell \sin \theta &\quad \Rightarrow \quad {\dot {x} }_{\mathrm {pend} }={\dot {x} }+\ell {\dot {\theta } }\cos \theta \\&y_{\mathrm {pend} }=-\ell \cos \theta &\quad \Rightarrow \quad {\dot {y} }_{\mathrm {pend} }=\ell {\dot {\theta } }\sin \theta .\end{array} }$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:40:21.382Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:40:21.382Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:34.115Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:34.115Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:14.477Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:14.477Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:01.281Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:01.281Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:57:16.361Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:57:16.361Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:37:07.578Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:37:07.578Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:15.405Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:15.405Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:08.667Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:08.667Z-->

{@{The generalized coordinates}@} can be taken to be {@{$x$ and $\theta$}@}. {@{The kinetic energy of the system}@} is then {@{$$T={\frac {1}{2} }M{\dot {x} }^{2}+{\frac {1}{2} }m\left({\dot {x} }_{\mathrm {pend} }^{2}+{\dot {y} }_{\mathrm {pend} }^{2}\right)$$}@} and {@{the potential energy}@} is {@{$$V=mgy_{\mathrm {pend} }$$ (annotation: recall this is the gravitational potential energy)}@} giving {@{the Lagrangian}@} {@{$${\begin{array}{rcl}L&=&T-V\\&=&{\frac {1}{2} }M{\dot {x} }^{2}+{\frac {1}{2} }m\left[\left({\dot {x} }+\ell {\dot {\theta } }\cos \theta \right)^{2}+\left(\ell {\dot {\theta } }\sin \theta \right)^{2}\right]+mg\ell \cos \theta \\&=&{\frac {1}{2} }\left(M+m\right){\dot {x} }^{2}+m{\dot {x} }\ell {\dot {\theta } }\cos \theta +{\frac {1}{2} }m\ell ^{2}{\dot {\theta } }^{2}+mg\ell \cos \theta .\end{array} }$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:50.575Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:50.575Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:25.836Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:25.836Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:56:42.558Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:56:42.558Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:51.567Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:51.567Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:42:25.309Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:42:25.309Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:27:10.341Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:27:10.341Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:34.118Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:34.118Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T19:06:23.987Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T19:06:23.987Z-->

(annotation: the Lagrangian: {@{$L = {\frac {1}{2} }\left(M+m\right){\dot {x} }^{2}+m{\dot {x} }\ell {\dot {\theta } }\cos \theta +{\frac {1}{2} }m\ell ^{2}{\dot {\theta } }^{2}+mg\ell \cos \theta$}@}) Since {@{_x_ is absent from the Lagrangian}@}, it is {@{a cyclic coordinate}@}. {@{The conserved momentum}@} is {@{$$p_{x}={\frac {\partial L}{\partial {\dot {x} } } }=(M+m){\dot {x} }+m\ell {\dot {\theta } }\cos \theta ,$$}@} and {@{the Lagrange equation for the support coordinate $x$}@} is {@{$$(M+m){\ddot {x} }+m\ell {\ddot {\theta } }\cos \theta -m\ell {\dot {\theta } }^{2}\sin \theta =0.$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:42:21.384Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:42:21.384Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:28:23.842Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:28:23.842Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:35.469Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:35.469Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:46.282Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:46.282Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:50.117Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:50.117Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:26:40.526Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:26:40.526Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:40:14.875Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:40:14.875Z-->

(annotation: the Lagrangian: {@{$L = {\frac {1}{2} }\left(M+m\right){\dot {x} }^{2}+m{\dot {x} }\ell {\dot {\theta } }\cos \theta +{\frac {1}{2} }m\ell ^{2}{\dot {\theta } }^{2}+mg\ell \cos \theta$}@}) {@{The Lagrange equation for the angle _θ_}@} is {@{$${\frac {\mathrm {d} }{\mathrm {d} t} }\left[m({\dot {x} }\ell \cos \theta +\ell ^{2}{\dot {\theta } })\right]+m\ell ({\dot {x} }{\dot {\theta } }+g)\sin \theta =0;$$}@} and simplifying {@{$${\ddot {\theta } }+{\frac {\ddot {x} }{\ell } }\cos \theta +{\frac {g}{\ell } }\sin \theta =0.$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:09.691Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:09.691Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:01:35.646Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:01:35.646Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:39:52.876Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:39:52.876Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:51.936Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:51.936Z-->

{@{These equations}@} may {@{look quite complicated}@}, but {@{finding them with Newton's laws}@} would have required {@{carefully identifying all forces}@}, which would have been {@{much more laborious and prone to errors}@}. By {@{considering limit cases}@}, {@{the correctness of this system can be verified}@}: For example, {@{${\ddot {x} }\to 0$}@} should give {@{the equations of motion for a [simple pendulum](simple%20pendulum.md)}@} that is {@{at rest in some [inertial frame](inertial%20frame.md)}@}, while {@{${\ddot {\theta } }\to 0$}@} should give {@{the equations for a pendulum in a constantly accelerating system, etc.}@} Furthermore, it is {@{trivial to obtain the results numerically}@}, given suitable {@{starting conditions and a chosen time step}@}, by {@{[stepping through the results iteratively](numerical%20ordinary%20differential%20equations.md)}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:25.498Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:25.498Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:10:57.740Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:10:57.740Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:42:18.514Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:42:18.514Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:46.375Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:46.375Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:40:48.430Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:40:48.430Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:08.312Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:08.312Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:31:19.277Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:31:19.277Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:04.882Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:04.882Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:54:01.982Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:54:01.982Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:40:17.836Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:40:17.836Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:27:04.591Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:27:04.591Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:25.782Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:25.782Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:36.866Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:36.866Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:25.382Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:25.382Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:18.761Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:18.761Z-->

### two-body central force problem

- Main articles: ::@:: [Two-body problem](two-body%20problem.md) and [Central force](central%20force.md) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:08:07.681Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:08:07.681Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:38:05.786Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:38:05.786Z-->

{@{Two bodies of masses _m_<sub>1</sub> and _m_<sub>2</sub>}@} with {@{position vectors __r__<sub>1</sub> and __r__<sub>2</sub>}@} are {@{in orbit about each other due to an attractive [central potential](central%20potential.md) _V_}@}. We may write down {@{the Lagrangian in terms of the position coordinates as they are}@}, but it is {@{an established procedure to convert the two-body problem into a one-body problem}@} as follows. Introduce {@{the [Jacobi coordinates](Jacobi%20coordinates.md)}@}; {@{the separation of the bodies __r__ = __r__<sub>2</sub> − __r__<sub>1</sub>}@} and {@{the location of the [center of mass](center%20of%20mass.md) __R__ = \(_m_<sub>1</sub>__r__<sub>1</sub> + _m_<sub>2</sub>__r__<sub>2</sub>\)/\(_m_<sub>1</sub> + _m_<sub>2</sub>\)}@}. {@{The Lagrangian}@} is then<sup>[\[44\]](#^ref-44)</sup><sup>[\[45\]](#^ref-45)</sup><sup>[\[nb 4\]](#^nb-4)</sup> {@{$$L=\underbrace { {\frac {1}{2} }M{\dot {\mathbf {R} } }^{2} } _{L_{\text{cm} } }+\underbrace { {\frac {1}{2} }\mu {\dot {\mathbf {r} } }^{2}-V(|\mathbf {r} |)} _{L_{\text{rel} } }$$}@} where {@{_M_ = _m_<sub>1</sub> + _m_<sub>2</sub> is the total mass}@}, {@{_μ_ = _m_<sub>1</sub>_m_<sub>2</sub>/\(_m_<sub>1</sub> + _m_<sub>2</sub>\) is the [reduced mass](reduced%20mass.md) (annotation: half of the harmonic mean)}@}, and {@{_V_ the potential of the radial force}@}, which depends {@{only on the [magnitude](norm%20(mathematics).md) of the separation \|__r__\| = \|__r__<sub>2</sub> − __r__<sub>1</sub>\|}@}. {@{The Lagrangian}@} splits into {@{a _center-of-mass_ term _L_<sub>cm</sub> and a _relative motion_ term _L_<sub>rel</sub>}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:40.181Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:40.181Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:08.808Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:08.808Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:20.356Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:20.356Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:08.788Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:08.788Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:01:54.181Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:01:54.181Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:24.828Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:24.828Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:11:22.159Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:11:22.159Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:37:15.061Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:37:15.061Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:52:29.492Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:52:29.492Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:50:25.673Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:50:25.673Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:45.076Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:45.076Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:28:21.419Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:28:21.419Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:40:37.055Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:40:37.055Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:11.834Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:11.834Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:25.618Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:25.618Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:43:48.780Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:43:48.780Z-->

(annotation: the Lagrangian: {@{$L=\underbrace { {\frac {1}{2} }M{\dot {\mathbf {R} } }^{2} } _{L_{\text{cm} } }+\underbrace { {\frac {1}{2} }\mu {\dot {\mathbf {r} } }^{2}-V(|\mathbf {r} |)} _{L_{\text{rel} } }$}@}) {@{The Euler–Lagrange equation for __R__}@} is simply {@{$$M{\ddot {\mathbf {R} } }=0,$$}@} which states {@{the center of mass}@} moves {@{in a straight line at constant velocity}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:21.428Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:21.428Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:42:00.484Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:42:00.484Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:10.049Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:10.049Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:48:35.589Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:48:35.589Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:45.496Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:45.496Z-->

(annotation: the Lagrangian: {@{$L=\underbrace { {\frac {1}{2} }M{\dot {\mathbf {R} } }^{2} } _{L_{\text{cm} } }+\underbrace { {\frac {1}{2} }\mu {\dot {\mathbf {r} } }^{2}-V(|\mathbf {r} |)} _{L_{\text{rel} } }$}@}) Since {@{the relative motion}@} only {@{depends on the magnitude of the separation}@}, it is ideal to use {@{polar coordinates \(_r_, _θ_\) and take _r_ = \|__r__\|}@}, {@{$$L_{\text{rel} }={\frac {1}{2} }\mu \left({\dot {r} }^{2}+r^{2}{\dot {\theta } }^{2}\right)-V(r),$$}@} so {@{_θ_ is a cyclic coordinate with the corresponding conserved \(angular\) momentum}@} {@{$$p_{\theta }={\frac {\partial L_{\text{rel} } }{\partial {\dot {\theta } } } }=\mu r^{2}{\dot {\theta } }=\ell .$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:58.137Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:58.137Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:47:20.612Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:47:20.612Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:00.456Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:00.456Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:11.900Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:11.900Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:40:33.806Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:40:33.806Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:28:07.336Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:28:07.336Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:51:47.901Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:51:47.901Z-->

(annotation: the Lagrangian of the relative motion: {@{$L_{\text{rel} }={\frac {1}{2} }\mu \left({\dot {r} }^{2}+r^{2}{\dot {\theta } }^{2}\right)-V(r)$}@}) {@{The radial coordinate _r_ and angular velocity d<!-- markdown separator -->_θ_<!-- markdown separator -->/d<!-- markdown separator -->_t_}@} can {@{vary with time}@}, but only in {@{such a way that _ℓ_ is constant}@}. {@{The Lagrange equation for _r_}@} is {@{$$\mu r{\dot {\theta } }^{2}-{\frac {dV}{dr} }=\mu {\ddot {r} }.$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:13:53.290Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:13:53.290Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:39:56.641Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:39:56.641Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:50:16.007Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:50:16.007Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:54:05.411Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:54:05.411Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:20:33.805Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:20:33.805Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:12:15.122Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:12:15.122Z-->

{@{This equation (annotation: the equation of motion: $\mu r{\dot {\theta } }^{2}-{\frac {dV}{dr} }=\mu {\ddot {r} }$)}@} is identical to {@{the radial equation obtained using Newton's laws in a _co-rotating_ reference frame}@}, that is, {@{a frame rotating with the reduced mass so it appears stationary}@}. Eliminating {@{the angular velocity d<!-- markdown separator -->_θ_<!-- markdown separator -->/d<!-- markdown separator -->_t_ from this radial equation (annotation: using angular momentum $\ell = \mu r^2 \dot \theta$)}@},<sup>[\[46\]](#^ref-46)</sup> {@{$$\mu {\ddot {r} }=-{\frac {\mathrm {d} V}{\mathrm {d} r} }+{\frac {\ell ^{2} }{\mu r^{3} } }.$$}@} which is {@{the equation of motion for a one-dimensional problem}@} in which {@{a particle of mass _μ_}@} is subjected to {@{the inward central force −d<!-- markdown separator -->_V_<!-- markdown separator -->/d<!-- markdown separator -->_r_}@} and {@{a second outward force, called in this context the _\(Lagrangian\) centrifugal force_}@} \(see {@{[centrifugal force\#Other uses of the term](centrifugal%20force.md#other%20uses%20of%20the%20term)}@}\): {@{$$F_{\mathrm {cf} }=\mu r{\dot {\theta } }^{2}={\frac {\ell ^{2} }{\mu r^{3} } }.$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:58.519Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:58.519Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:12:14.380Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:12:14.380Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:57:10.321Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:57:10.321Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:02.993Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:02.993Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:31:47.533Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:31:47.533Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:33:52.458Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:33:52.458Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:49.660Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:49.660Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:14.697Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:14.697Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:00.955Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:00.955Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:28.464Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:28.464Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:33.292Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:33.292Z-->

Of course, if one remains {@{entirely within the one-dimensional formulation}@}, {@{_ℓ_ enters only}@} as {@{some imposed parameter of the external outward force}@}, and its {@{interpretation as angular momentum}@} depends upon {@{the more general two-dimensional problem from which the one-dimensional problem originated}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:14.511Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:14.511Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:30:57.014Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:30:57.014Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:28:03.281Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:28:03.281Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:06:39.844Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:06:39.844Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:06.451Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:06.451Z-->

If one arrives at {@{this equation using Newtonian mechanics in a co-rotating frame}@}, {@{the interpretation is evident}@} as {@{the centrifugal force in that frame due to the rotation of the frame itself}@}. If one arrives at {@{this equation directly by using the generalized coordinates \(_r_, _θ_\)}@} and simply following {@{the Lagrangian formulation without thinking about frames at all}@}, {@{the interpretation}@} is that {@{the centrifugal force is an outgrowth of _using polar coordinates_}@}. As Hildebrand says:<sup>[\[47\]](#^ref-47)</sup> (annotation: about {@{inertia forces and coordinate system}@}) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:58.846Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:58.846Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:27.360Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:27.360Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:01.021Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:01.021Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:28:03.333Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:28:03.333Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:48.347Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:48.347Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:36.841Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:36.841Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:59.262Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:59.262Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:28:08.125Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:28:08.125Z-->

"Since {@{such quantities}@} are not {@{true physical forces}@}, they are often called {@{_inertia forces_}@}. Their {@{presence or absence}@} depends, {@{not upon the particular problem at hand, but _upon the coordinate system chosen_}@}." In particular, if {@{Cartesian coordinates are chosen}@}, {@{the centrifugal force disappears}@}, and the formulation involves {@{only the central force itself}@}, which provides {@{the [centripetal force](centripetal%20force.md) for a curved motion}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:34:47.300Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:34:47.300Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:29.048Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:29.048Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:46.823Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:46.823Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:28:14.808Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:28:14.808Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:22.455Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:22.455Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:38:54.327Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:38:54.327Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:18.523Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:18.523Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:33:54.296Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:33:54.296Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:28.871Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:28.871Z-->

This {@{viewpoint, that fictitious forces originate in the choice of coordinates}@}, often is expressed by {@{users of the Lagrangian method}@}. This view arises {@{naturally in the Lagrangian approach}@}, because {@{the frame of reference}@} is {@{\(possibly unconsciously\) selected by the choice of coordinates}@}. For example, see<sup>[\[48\]](#^ref-48)</sup> for {@{a comparison of Lagrangians in an inertial and in a noninertial frame of reference}@}. See also the discussion of {@{"total" and "updated" Lagrangian formulations}@} in.<sup>[\[49\]](#^ref-49)</sup> Unfortunately, {@{this usage of "inertial force"}@} conflicts with {@{the Newtonian idea of an inertial force}@}. In {@{the Newtonian view}@}, {@{an inertial force}@} originates in {@{the acceleration of the frame of observation}@} \(the fact that it is not {@{an [inertial frame of reference](inertial%20frame%20of%20reference.md)}@}\), not in {@{the choice of coordinate system}@}. To keep {@{matters clear}@}, it is safest to refer to {@{the Lagrangian inertial forces as _generalized_ inertial forces}@}, to distinguish them from {@{the Newtonian vector inertial forces}@}. That is, one should avoid {@{following Hildebrand when he says}@} \(p. 155\) "we deal {@{_always_ with _generalized_ forces, velocities accelerations, and momenta}@}. For {@{brevity, the adjective "generalized"}@} will be {@{omitted frequently}@}." <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:24.960Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:24.960Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:03.834Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:03.834Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:38.799Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:38.799Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:56.121Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:56.121Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:34:44.533Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:34:44.533Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:44.543Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:44.543Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:37:08.670Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:37:08.670Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:28:22.182Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:28:22.182Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:31:49.954Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:31:49.954Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:39.673Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:39.673Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:29.476Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:29.476Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:28.484Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:28.484Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:53.245Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:53.245Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:55.326Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:55.326Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:33:48.311Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:33:48.311Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:48.934Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:48.934Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:48:32.914Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:48:32.914Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:50:07.376Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:50:07.376Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:47:18.700Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:47:18.700Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:43.635Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:43.635Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:49:55.269Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:49:55.269Z-->

It is known that {@{the Lagrangian of a system}@} is not {@{unique}@}. Within {@{the Lagrangian formalism}@} {@{the Newtonian fictitious forces}@} can be identified by {@{the existence of alternative Lagrangians in which the fictitious forces disappear}@}, sometimes found by {@{exploiting the symmetry of the system}@}.<sup>[\[50\]](#^ref-50)</sup> <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:21.012Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:21.012Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:41:18.555Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:41:18.555Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:01.729Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:01.729Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:47.686Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:47.686Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:17.202Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:17.202Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:12.079Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:12.079Z-->

## extensions to include non-conservative forces

### dissipative forces

{@{[Dissipation](dissipation.md) \(i.e. non-conservative systems\)}@} can also be treated with {@{an effective Lagrangian}@} formulated by {@{a certain doubling of the degrees of freedom}@}.<sup>[\[51\]](#^ref-51)</sup><sup>[\[52\]](#^ref-52)</sup><sup>[\[53\]](#^ref-53)</sup><sup>[\[54\]](#^ref-54)</sup> <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-23T04:28:24.834Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T04:28:24.834Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:11.453Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:11.453Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:32:07.877Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:32:07.877Z-->

In {@{a more general formulation}@}, {@{the forces}@} could be {@{both conservative and [viscous](viscosity.md)}@}. If {@{an appropriate transformation}@} can be {@{found from the __F__<sub>_i_</sub>}@}, {@{[Rayleigh](John%20Strutt,%203rd%20Baron%20Rayleigh.md) suggests using a [dissipation function](Rayleigh%20dissipation%20function.md), _D_}@}, of the following form:<sup>[\[55\]](#^ref-55)</sup> {@{$$D={\frac {1}{2} }\sum _{j=1}^{m}\sum _{k=1}^{m}C_{jk}{\dot {q} }_{j}{\dot {q} }_{k},$$}@} where {@{_C_<sub>_jk_</sub> are constants that are related to the damping coefficients in the physical system}@}, though {@{not necessarily equal to them}@}. (annotation: It can be interpreted as {@{half the squared norm of the generalized velocity vector $\dot{q}$}@} under {@{the inner product defined by the damping metric tensor $C_{jk}$ on the tangent space $T_q Q$}@}.) If {@{_D_ is defined this way}@}, then<sup>[\[55\]](#^ref-55)</sup> {@{$$Q_{j}=-{\frac {\partial V}{\partial q_{j} } }-{\frac {\partial D}{\partial {\dot {q} }_{j} } }$$}@} (annotation: Interpreted as {@{the force is reduced by the dissipation}@}. Also note the suggestive {@{$\frac {\partial D} {\partial {\dot {q} }_{j} } = \sum_{k=1}^{m} C_{jk} \dot{q}_k$}@}.) and {@{$${\frac {\mathrm {d} }{\mathrm {d} t} }\left({\frac {\partial L}{\partial {\dot {q} }_{j} } }\right)-{\frac {\partial L}{\partial q_{j} } }+{\frac {\partial D}{\partial {\dot {q} }_{j} } }=0.$$}@} (annotation: Recall that {@{the second kind of Lagrange's equations}@} is derived by {@{subtracting two equations both individually equaling $Q_j$}@}. Repeat {@{the derivation with the above $Q_j$}@} and you get {@{the new Lagrange's equations with dissipative forces}@}.) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:09.900Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:09.900Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:48:15.043Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:48:15.043Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:10.128Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:10.128Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:32:10.055Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:32:10.055Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:13.482Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:13.482Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:47:30.628Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:47:30.628Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:38:56.841Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:38:56.841Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:57:44.419Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:57:44.419Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:43.200Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:43.200Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:56.365Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:56.365Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:07:26.495Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:07:26.495Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:18.962Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:18.962Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:34:40.674Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:34:40.674Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:32:44.978Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:32:44.978Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:35.830Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:35.830Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:04.927Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:04.927Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:17.485Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:17.485Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:25:54.151Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:25:54.151Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:41:13.442Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:41:13.442Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:00.014Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:00.014Z-->

### electromagnetism

{@{A test particle}@} is {@{a particle whose [mass](mass.md) and [charge](electric%20charge.md)}@} are {@{assumed to be so small that its effect on external system is insignificant}@}. It is often {@{a hypothetical simplified point particle}@} with no {@{properties other than mass and charge}@}. {@{Real particles like [electrons](electron.md) and [up quarks](up%20quark.md)}@} are {@{more complex and have additional terms in their Lagrangians}@}. Not only can {@{the fields}@} form {@{non conservative potentials}@}, {@{these potentials}@} can also be {@{velocity dependent}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:13.735Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:13.735Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:08:06.822Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:08:06.822Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:33.790Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:33.790Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:29:00.547Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:29:00.547Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:16.058Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:16.058Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:58.835Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:58.835Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:23.051Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:23.051Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:54.898Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:54.898Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:51.645Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:51.645Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:23.402Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:23.402Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:25:19.810Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:25:19.810Z-->

{@{The Lagrangian for a [charged particle](charged%20particle.md)}@} with {@{[electrical charge](electrical%20charge.md) _q_, interacting with an [electromagnetic field](electromagnetic%20field.md)}@}, is {@{the prototypical example of a velocity-dependent potential}@}. {@{The electric [scalar potential](scalar%20potential.md) _ϕ_ = _ϕ_\(__r__, _t_\)}@} and {@{[magnetic vector potential](magnetic%20vector%20potential.md) __A__ = __A__\(__r__, _t_\)}@} are defined from {@{the [electric field](electric%20field.md) __E__ = __E__\(__r__, _t_\) and [magnetic field](magnetic%20field.md) __B__ = __B__\(__r__, _t_\)}@} as follows: {@{$$\mathbf {E} =-{\boldsymbol {\nabla } }\phi -{\frac {\partial \mathbf {A} }{\partial t} },\quad \mathbf {B} ={\boldsymbol {\nabla } }\times \mathbf {A} .$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:25.941Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:25.941Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:24.402Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:24.402Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:46.660Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:46.660Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:14.405Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:14.405Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:17.342Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:17.342Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:13.887Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:13.887Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:48.350Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:48.350Z-->

{@{The Lagrangian of a massive charged test particle in an electromagnetic field}@} {@{$$L={\tfrac {1}{2} }m{\dot {\mathbf {r} } }^{2}+q\,{\dot {\mathbf {r} } }\cdot \mathbf {A} -q\phi ,$$}@} is called {@{[minimal coupling](minimal%20coupling.md)}@}. This is a good example of when {@{the common [rule of thumb](rule%20of%20thumb.md) that the Lagrangian is the kinetic energy minus the potential energy is incorrect}@}. Combined with {@{[Euler–Lagrange equation](Euler–Lagrange%20equation.md)}@}, it produces {@{the [Lorentz force](Lorentz%20force.md) law}@} {@{$$m{\ddot {\mathbf {r} } }=q\mathbf {E} +q{\dot {\mathbf {r} } }\times \mathbf {B}$$}@} (annotation: Applying {@{the Euler–Lagrange equation $\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial L}{\partial \mathbf{\dot{r} } } - \frac{\partial L}{\partial \mathbf{r} } = 0$}@} (remembering to apply {@{the chain rule for the total time derivative}@} {@{$\frac{d}{dt} = \frac{\partial}{\partial t} + (\mathbf{\dot{r} }\cdot\nabla)$ to the vector potential term $\mathbf{A}(t, \mathbf{r})$}@} rather than treating {@{position as independent of time}@}) to {@{$L = \frac{1}{2}m\mathbf{\dot{r} }^2 + q\mathbf{\dot{r} }\cdot\mathbf{A} - q\phi$}@}, {@{the momentum derivative}@} {@{$\frac{d}{dt}(m\mathbf{\dot{r} } + q\mathbf{A}) = m\mathbf{\ddot{r} } + q\frac{\partial \mathbf{A} }{\partial t} + q(\mathbf{\dot{r} }\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{A}$}@} equates to {@{the spatial gradient}@} {@{$\frac{\partial L}{\partial \mathbf{r} } = q\nabla(\mathbf{\dot{r} }\cdot\mathbf{A}) - q\nabla\phi$}@} (where {@{spatial derivatives}@} act {@{strictly on $\mathbf{A}$ and $\phi$}@}, with $\mathbf{\dot{r} }$ treated as {@{an independent velocity vector field coordinate}@}); utilizing {@{the vector identity $\nabla(\mathbf{\dot{r} }\cdot\mathbf{A}) - (\mathbf{\dot{r} }\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{A} = \mathbf{\dot{r} }\times(\nabla\times\mathbf{A})$}@} then yields {@{the Lorentz force law $m\mathbf{\ddot{r} } = q\mathbf{E} + q\mathbf{\dot{r} }\times\mathbf{B}$}@}.) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:52.917Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:52.917Z!fsrs,2026-08-29T06:57:24.736Z,12,11.6874828,1,2,2,0,0,2026-08-17T06:57:24.736Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:39.673Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:39.673Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:28:22.729Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:28:22.729Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:21:29.467Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:21:29.467Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:57:04.154Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:57:04.154Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:08:44.598Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:08:44.598Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:52:28.505Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:52:28.505Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:16.002Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:16.002Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:27:40.005Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:27:40.005Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:48:33.471Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:48:33.471Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:18.970Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:18.970Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:29:41.479Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:29:41.479Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:36:50.071Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:36:50.071Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:31.225Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:31.225Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:34:46.677Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:34:46.677Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:06.964Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:06.964Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:19.562Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:19.562Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:10.285Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:10.285Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T04:29:51.637Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T04:29:51.637Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:27:32.396Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:27:32.396Z-->

Under {@{[gauge transformation](gauge%20transformation.md)}@}: {@{$$\mathbf {A} \rightarrow \mathbf {A} +{\boldsymbol {\nabla } }f,\quad \phi \rightarrow \phi -{\dot {f} },$$}@} where {@{_f_\(__r__,_t_\) is any scalar function of space and time}@}, {@{the aforementioned Lagrangian}@} transforms like: {@{$$L\rightarrow L+q\left({\dot {\mathbf {r} } }\cdot {\boldsymbol {\nabla } }+{\frac {\partial }{\partial t} }\right)f=L+q{\frac {df}{dt} },$$}@} which still produces {@{the same Lorentz force law (annotation: since the Lagrangian differs by the total time derivative of a function that depends only on position and time)}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:00:56.068Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:00:56.068Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:18.121Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:18.121Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:29:53.025Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:29:53.025Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:34:34.522Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:34:34.522Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:30:56.137Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:30:56.137Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:06:48.245Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:06:48.245Z-->

Note that {@{the [canonical momentum](canonical%20momentum.md) \(conjugate to position __r__\)}@} is {@{the [kinetic momentum](kinetic%20momentum.md#particle%20in%20field) plus a contribution from the __A__ field \(known as the potential momentum\)}@}: {@{$$\mathbf {p} ={\frac {\partial L}{\partial {\dot {\mathbf {r} } } } }=m{\dot {\mathbf {r} } }+q\mathbf {A} .$$}@} <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:12.772Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:12.772Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T19:06:14.167Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T19:06:14.167Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:11:25.239Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:11:25.239Z-->

{@{This relation (annotation: canonical momentum = kinetic momentum + potential momentum)}@} is also used in {@{the [minimal coupling](minimal%20coupling.md) prescription}@} in {@{[quantum mechanics](quantum%20mechanics.md) and [quantum field theory](quantum%20field%20theory.md)}@}. From {@{this expression (annotation: transforming $\mathbf A$ changes $\mathbf p$)}@}, we can see that {@{the [canonical momentum](canonical%20momentum.md) __p__}@} is not {@{gauge invariant, and therefore not a measurable physical quantity}@}; However, if {@{__r__ is cyclic \(i.e. Lagrangian is independent of position __r__\)}@}, which happens if {@{the _ϕ_ and __A__ fields are uniform}@}, then {@{this canonical momentum __p__ given here is the conserved momentum}@}, while {@{the measurable physical kinetic momentum _m_<!-- markdown separator -->__v__ is not}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:12.023Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:12.023Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:44.767Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:44.767Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:38.373Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:38.373Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:33.211Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:33.211Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:28.730Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:28.730Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:56.203Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:56.203Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:09:16.343Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:09:16.343Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:26.805Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:26.805Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:40:43.675Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:40:43.675Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:19.361Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:19.361Z-->

## other contexts and formulations

{@{The ideas in Lagrangian mechanics}@} have {@{numerous applications in other areas of physics}@}, and can adopt {@{generalized results from the calculus of variations}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:13:17.920Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:13:17.920Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:12.561Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:12.561Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:15.209Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:15.209Z-->

### alternative formulations of classical mechanics

{@{A closely related formulation of classical mechanics}@} is {@{[Hamiltonian mechanics](Hamiltonian%20mechanics.md)}@}. {@{The Hamiltonian}@} is defined by {@{$$H=\sum _{i=1}^{n}{\dot {q} }_{i}{\frac {\partial L}{\partial {\dot {q} }_{i} } }-L$$}@} and can be obtained by performing {@{a [Legendre transformation](Legendre%20transformation.md) on the Lagrangian}@}, which introduces new {@{variables [canonically conjugate](canonical%20coordinates.md) to the original variables}@}. For example, given {@{a set of generalized coordinates}@}, {@{the variables [canonically conjugate](canonical%20coordinates.md)}@} are {@{the generalized momenta}@}. This doubles {@{the number of variables}@}, but makes {@{differential equations first order}@}. {@{The Hamiltonian}@} is a particularly {@{ubiquitous quantity in [quantum mechanics](quantum%20mechanics.md)}@} \(see {@{[Hamiltonian \(quantum mechanics\)](Hamiltonian%20(quantum%20mechanics).md)}@}\). <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:27.852Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:27.852Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:34:43.331Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:34:43.331Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:23.385Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:23.385Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:19.430Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:19.430Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:49.576Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:49.576Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:27.949Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:27.949Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:55.572Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:55.572Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:00:58.251Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:00:58.251Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:57:18.687Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:57:18.687Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:45.910Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:45.910Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:53.995Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:53.995Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:10:48.249Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:10:48.249Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:30:54.888Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:30:54.888Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:25:50.211Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:25:50.211Z-->

{@{[Routhian mechanics](Routhian%20mechanics.md)}@} is {@{a hybrid formulation of Lagrangian and Hamiltonian mechanics}@}, which is not {@{often used in practice}@} but {@{an efficient formulation for cyclic coordinates}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:50.317Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:50.317Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:38:48.209Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:38:48.209Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:07.002Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:07.002Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:15.207Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:15.207Z-->

### momentum space formulation

- Main article: ::@:: [Position and momentum space § Lagrangian mechanics](position%20and%20momentum%20space.md#Lagrangian%20mechanics) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:28:16.993Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:28:16.993Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:20.495Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:20.495Z-->

{@{The Euler–Lagrange equations}@} can also be formulated in terms of {@{the generalized momenta rather than generalized coordinates}@}. Performing {@{a Legendre transformation on the generalized coordinate Lagrangian _L_\(__q__, d<!-- markdown separator -->__q__<!-- markdown separator -->/d<!-- markdown separator -->_t_, _t_\)}@} obtains {@{the generalized momenta Lagrangian _L_′\(__p__, d<!-- markdown separator -->__p__<!-- markdown separator -->/d<!-- markdown separator -->_t_, _t_\) in terms of the original Lagrangian}@}, as well {@{the EL equations in terms of the generalized momenta}@}. Both {@{Lagrangians contain the same information}@}, and either can be {@{used to solve for the motion of the system}@}. In practice {@{generalized coordinates}@} are {@{more convenient to use and interpret than generalized momenta}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:53.599Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:53.599Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:47:22.229Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:47:22.229Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:57:20.036Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:57:20.036Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:29:39.229Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:29:39.229Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:47:29.771Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:47:29.771Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:35:09.121Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:35:09.121Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:19.561Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:19.561Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:31.254Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:31.254Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:41.861Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:41.861Z-->

### alternative Lagrangians

Since {@{Lagrangians that differ by a total time derivative term ${\frac {df(x,t)}{dt} }$}@} produce {@{the same equations of motion}@},<sup>[\[56\]](#^ref-56)</sup> it is possible to write {@{the traditional one-dimensional Lagrangian, $L=T-U$}@}, in the equivalent form {@{$$L_{a}=\left({\sqrt {T} }\pm i{\sqrt {U} }\right)^{2}$$}@} (annotation: Expanding {@{$L_a = \left(\sqrt{T} \pm i\sqrt{U}\right)^2 = \left(\sqrt{\frac{m}{2} }\dot{x} \pm i\sqrt{U}\right)^2$}@} yields {@{the traditional Lagrangian $T - U$}@} plus {@{the imaginary cross-term $\pm i\dot{x}\sqrt{2mU}$}@}. Because {@{this cross-term ($U$ depends on $x$ only)}@} is precisely {@{the total time derivative $\frac{d}{dt}\left[\pm i\int \sqrt{2mU}\,dx\right]$}@}, it {@{drops out under the Euler–Lagrange equations}@}, leaving {@{the resulting equations of motion identical}@}.) where it is assumed that, {@{everywhere between the fixed endpoints}@}, {@{the kinetic energy}@} is of the form {@{$T=a(x){\dot {x} }^{2}\geq 0$ and $U(x)\geq 0$}@}. For {@{a kinetic energy $T=m{\dot {x} }^{2}/2\geq 0$}@}, {@{the resulting equation of motion}@} is {@{$${\sqrt {\frac {m}{2} } }{\dot {x} }\pm i{\sqrt {U} }=A\exp \left[\pm i{\sqrt {\frac {2}{m} } }\int {\frac {\partial {\sqrt {U} } }{\partial x} }dt\right]$$}@} (annotation: Define {@{the complex function $\Psi(x,\dot{x}) = \sqrt{\frac{m}{2} }\dot{x} \pm i\sqrt{U(x)}$}@}. Substituting {@{$L_a$ into the Euler–Lagrange equation $\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial L_a}{\partial \dot{x} }\right) - \frac{\partial L_a}{\partial x} = 0$}@} with {@{the partial derivatives $\frac{\partial L_a}{\partial \dot{x} } = 2\Psi\sqrt{\frac{m}{2} }$ and $\frac{\partial L_a}{\partial x} = \pm 2i\Psi \frac{\partial \sqrt{U} }{\partial x}$}@} yields {@{the differential equation $\sqrt{\frac{m}{2} }\frac{d\Psi}{dt} = \pm i \frac{\partial \sqrt{U} }{\partial x}\Psi$}@}. {@{Separating variables}@} gives {@{$\frac{d\Psi}{\Psi} = \pm i \sqrt{\frac{2}{m} }\frac{\partial \sqrt{U} }{\partial x} dt$}@}, which integrates to {@{$\ln \Psi = \pm i \sqrt{\frac{2}{m} } \int \frac{\partial \sqrt{U} }{\partial x} dt + \text{constant}$}@}. {@{Exponentiating both sides}@} delivers {@{the desired first-order phasor equation above}@}, where {@{$A$ is the integration constant corresponding to the initial condition}@} with its {@{square equaling the conserved total mechanical energy $\lvert A \rvert^2 = \lvert \Psi \rvert^2 = T + U = E$}@}.) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:57:04.890Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:57:04.890Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:32:13.327Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:32:13.327Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:28:09.741Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:28:09.741Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:31.544Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:31.544Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:50:12.419Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:50:12.419Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:35:59.222Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:35:59.222Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:42.167Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:42.167Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:40:43.559Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:40:43.559Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:30.184Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:30.184Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:27:01.255Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:27:01.255Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:06.648Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:06.648Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:40:39.159Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:40:39.159Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:33:55.634Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:33:55.634Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:17.563Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:17.563Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:40.952Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:40.952Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:32:50.940Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:32:50.940Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:29:13.062Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:29:13.062Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:29:30.420Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:29:30.420Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:28.151Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:28.151Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:38:52.985Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:38:52.985Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:22.390Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:22.390Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:32.636Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:32.636Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:10:39.247Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:10:39.247Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:36:35.605Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:36:35.605Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:16.811Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:16.811Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:53.744Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:53.744Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:05.539Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:05.539Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:33.479Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:33.479Z-->

{@{This equation (annotation: ${\sqrt {\frac {m}{2} } }{\dot {x} }\pm i{\sqrt {U} }=A\exp \left[\pm i{\sqrt {\frac {2}{m} } }\int {\frac {\partial {\sqrt {U} } }{\partial x} }dt\right]$)}@} provides {@{a [phasor](phasor.md) picture of the energy of the system}@}. The phasor has {@{a magnitude equal to the square root of the total energy}@}, and it rotates {@{between the ${\sqrt {\frac {m}{2} } }{\dot {x} }$ and ${\sqrt {U} }$ axes at an angle ${\sqrt {\frac {2}{m} } }\int {\frac {\partial {\sqrt {U} } }{\partial x} }dt$}@}. Thus {@{the transfer of energy from potential to kinetic}@} is conveyed by {@{rotations of the phasor}@}.<sup>[\[57\]](#^ref-57)</sup> <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:10:47.870Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:10:47.870Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:50.482Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:50.482Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:33.320Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:33.320Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:47.900Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:47.900Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:21.226Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:21.226Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:38:47.241Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:38:47.241Z-->

### alternative actions

It is possible to show, using {@{[Lagrange multiplier](Lagrange%20multiplier.md) techniques<sup>[\[58\]](#^ref-58)</sup> and other techniques}@},<sup>[\[59\]](#^ref-59)</sup><sup>[\[60\]](#^ref-60)</sup> that {@{many alternative actions}@} may be used to produce {@{the same equations of motion as the traditional action}@}. {@{These alternative actions}@} are typically formulated with {@{the assumption that time averaged values of the total energy are fixed}@}. It is also possible to assume that {@{time-averaged values of the kinetic or potential energies are fixed}@}. For example, consider {@{the actions (annotation: recall the traditional action is $S=\int L\,dt=\int (T-U)\,dt$)}@} {@{$$S_{T}=\int T\,dt+\lambda _{T}\left[\int \left(T+U\right)\,dt-{\bar {E} }\Delta t\right],$$}@} and {@{$$S_{U}=\int U\,dt+\lambda _{U}\left[\int \left(T+U\right)\,dt-{\bar {E} }\Delta t\right].$$}@} {@{The terms in the square brackets}@} represent {@{the constraint}@} that {@{the time-averaged value of $T+U$ over the interval $\Delta t$ must be ${\bar {E} }$}@}. {@{Both actions}@} produce {@{the same equations of motion as each other and as the traditional action}@} for {@{$\lambda _{T}=-1/2$ and $\lambda _{U}=-1/2$ (annotation: note the $\lambda$-s are functions of $t$ in general)}@}, (annotation: They both {@{differ from the traditional action by the constant $-\frac 1 2 \bar E \Delta t$}@}.) which are also {@{the values for which the total energy $T+U$ is at all times}@} equal to {@{the time-averaged energy ${\bar {E} }$ (annotation: the time interval $\Delta t$ can be set arbitrarily)}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:08.704Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:08.704Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:29:55.124Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:29:55.124Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:30.549Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:30.549Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:31:13.217Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:31:13.217Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:21.089Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:21.089Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:37.520Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:37.520Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T04:29:55.353Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T04:29:55.353Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:41:41.992Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:41:41.992Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:18.905Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:18.905Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:57.477Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:57.477Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:48:38.128Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:48:38.128Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:16.046Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:16.046Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:12.813Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:12.813Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:01.009Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:01.009Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:01:58.181Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:01:58.181Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:57.048Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:57.048Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:24.667Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:24.667Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:33.334Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:33.334Z-->

{@{One interesting feature of $S_{T}$}@} is that its {@{extremal value is a minimum value}@} in {@{the typical case where $T={\frac {1}{2} }m{\dot {x} }^{2}$}@}. This is true since {@{the square bracketed constraint term in the action}@} is, {@{by definition, constant (annotation: constraint functions are set to zero)}@}, and {@{the second variation of the action}@} will have {@{the form $\delta ^{2}S_{T}=\int m{\dot {\eta } }^{2}\,dt$}@}, where {@{$\eta$ is the variation of the path}@}. (annotation: Substitute {@{$q \to q + \eta$ into the alternative action $S_T = \int T\, dt$}@}, expand {@{the kinetic energy $T$ in a Taylor series}@} {@{$T(\dot{q} + \dot{\eta}) = T(\dot{q}) + \frac{\partial T}{\partial \dot{q} }\dot{\eta} + \frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^2 T}{\partial \dot{q}^2}\dot{\eta}^2 + \mathcal{O}(\dot{\eta}^3)$}@}, and isolate {@{the second-order perturbation term in $\dot{\eta}$}@} to obtain {@{$\delta^2 S_T = \frac{1}{2} \int \frac{\partial^2 T}{\partial \dot{q}^2} \dot{\eta}^2 dt = \int m \dot{\eta}^2 dt$}@}.) Clearly {@{$\delta ^{2}S_{T}\geq 0$ for any $\eta$}@}, so {@{the extremal value must be a minimum}@}.<sup>[\[61\]](#^ref-61)</sup> (annotation: Analogous to {@{$f''(x) \ge 0$ in single-variable calculus}@}, {@{a positive second variation ($\delta^2 S_T \ge 0$)}@} guarantees {@{the stationary path minimizes the action}@}.) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:40.596Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:40.596Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:37:05.654Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:37:05.654Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:46.559Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:46.559Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:34:42.476Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:34:42.476Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:14:20.402Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:14:20.402Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:41:19.535Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:41:19.535Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:28:53.238Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:28:53.238Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:33.838Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:33.838Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:53.137Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:53.137Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:43.136Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:43.136Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:33:19.199Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:33:19.199Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:13.386Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:13.386Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:57:07.235Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:57:07.235Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:35.023Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:35.023Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:36.272Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:36.272Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:28:46.482Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:28:46.482Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:51.448Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:51.448Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:25:35.755Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:25:35.755Z-->

Additionally, one might consider actions consisting of {@{products of time-integrals of energy}@}. For example {@{an product action with a constraint on the time-averaged energy}@} {@{$$S_{P}=\left[\int T\,dt\right]\left[\int U\,dt\right]+\lambda _{P}\left[\int \left(T+U\right)\,dt-{\bar {E} }\Delta t\right]$$}@} will produce {@{the same equations of motion as the traditional action}@}, for {@{the appropriate choice of $\lambda _{P}$}@}. (annotation: To mirror {@{Hamilton's $I_T - I_U$ structure}@}, {@{the effective weight of $I_T$ ($W_T = I_U + \lambda_P$) and $I_U$ ($W_U = I_T + \lambda_P$)}@} must be {@{equal in magnitude and opposite in sign}@}. Setting {@{$W_T = -W_U$}@} directly yields {@{$\lambda_P = -\frac{I_T + I_U}{2}$}@}.) It is also possible to produce {@{the traditional equations of motion for powers of products of time-integrated energies}@}, such as {@{$$S_{nm}=\left[\int T\,dt\right]^{n}\left[\int U\,dt\right]^{m}+\lambda _{nm}\left[\int \left(T+U\right)\,dt-{\bar {E} }\Delta t\right]$$}@} so long as {@{the appropriate value of $\lambda _{nm}$ is chosen}@}.<sup>[\[58\]](#^ref-58)</sup> (annotation: {@{Differentiating $I_T^n I_U^m$}@} gives {@{effective weights $W_T = n I_T^{n-1} I_U^m + \lambda_{nm}$ and $W_U = m I_T^n I_U^{m-1} + \lambda_{nm}$}@}. Imposing {@{equal and opposite weighting ($W_T = -W_U$)}@} isolates {@{$\lambda_{nm} = -\frac{1}{2} I_T^{n-1} I_U^{m-1} (n I_U + m I_T)$}@}.) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:28:16.020Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:28:16.020Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:32.326Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:32.326Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:14.247Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:14.247Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T04:20:00.224Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T04:20:00.224Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:05.874Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:05.874Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:11.177Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:11.177Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:07.471Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:07.471Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:48:40.029Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:48:40.029Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:26.967Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:26.967Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:36.492Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:36.492Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:18.767Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:18.767Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:13.125Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:13.125Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:47.402Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:47.402Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:35:08.056Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:35:08.056Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:13.972Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:13.972Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:27.858Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:27.858Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:42:49.122Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:42:49.122Z-->

### higher derivatives of generalized coordinates

There is no {@{mathematical reason}@} to restrict {@{the derivatives of generalized coordinates to first order only}@}. It is possible to derive {@{modified EL equations for a Lagrangian containing higher order derivatives}@}, see {@{[Euler–Lagrange equation](Euler–Lagrange%20equation.md)}@} for details. However, from {@{the physical point-of-view}@} there is {@{an obstacle to include time derivatives higher than the first order}@}, which is implied by {@{Ostrogradsky's construction of a canonical formalism}@} for {@{nondegenerate higher derivative Lagrangians}@}, see {@{[Ostrogradsky instability](Ostrogradsky%20instability.md)}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:31:57.161Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:31:57.161Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:04.584Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:04.584Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:37:33.747Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:37:33.747Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T06:28:05.596Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T06:28:05.596Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:52.623Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:52.623Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:41:17.269Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:41:17.269Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T06:25:13.946Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T06:25:13.946Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:39:30.214Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:39:30.214Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:01:56.828Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:01:56.828Z-->

### optics

- Main article: ::@:: [Hamiltonian optics](Hamiltonian%20optics.md) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:09:03.545Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:09:03.545Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:38.979Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:38.979Z-->

{@{Lagrangian mechanics}@} can be applied to {@{[geometrical optics](geometrical%20optics.md)}@}, by applying {@{variational principles to rays of light in a medium}@}, and solving {@{the EL equations}@} gives {@{the equations of the paths the light rays follow}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:47:28.084Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:47:28.084Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:34:30.845Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:34:30.845Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:53:24.349Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:53:24.349Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:44:23.890Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:44:23.890Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:57.403Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:57.403Z-->

### relativistic formulation

- Main article: ::@:: [Relativistic Lagrangian mechanics](relativistic%20Lagrangian%20mechanics.md) <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T05:59:53.164Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T05:59:53.164Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:22.422Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:22.422Z-->

{@{Lagrangian mechanics can be formulated}@} in {@{[special relativity](special%20relativity.md) and [general relativity](general%20relativity.md)}@}. {@{Some features of Lagrangian mechanics}@} are {@{retained in the relativistic theories}@} but difficulties {@{quickly appear in other respects}@}. In particular, {@{the EL equations}@} take {@{the same form}@}, and {@{the connection between cyclic coordinates and conserved momenta}@} still applies, however {@{the Lagrangian must be modified}@} and is not {@{simply the kinetic minus the potential energy of a particle}@}. Also, it is not {@{straightforward to handle multiparticle systems}@} in {@{a [manifestly covariant](manifestly%20covariant.md) way}@}, it may be possible if {@{a particular frame of reference is singled out}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:44.618Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:44.618Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:30:31.162Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:30:31.162Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:30:59.789Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:30:59.789Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:47.092Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:47.092Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:36:03.479Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:36:03.479Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:54:42.853Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:54:42.853Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:31:18.350Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:31:18.350Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:31:31.229Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:31:31.229Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:38:46.030Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:38:46.030Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:13:57.510Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:13:57.510Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:26:02.782Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:26:02.782Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:50:18.665Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:50:18.665Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:32.332Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:32.332Z-->

### quantum mechanics

In {@{[quantum mechanics](quantum%20mechanics.md)}@}, {@{[action](action%20(physics).md) and quantum-mechanical [phase](phase%20(waves).md)}@} are {@{related via the [Planck constant](Planck%20constant.md)}@}, and {@{the [principle of stationary action](principle%20of%20stationary%20action.md)}@} can be understood in terms of {@{[constructive interference](constructive%20interference.md) of [wave functions](wave%20function.md)}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:04.387Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:04.387Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:43:45.877Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:43:45.877Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:08:09.159Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:08:09.159Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:57.635Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:57.635Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:08:59.188Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:08:59.188Z-->

In {@{1948, [Feynman](Richard%20Feynman.md) discovered the [path integral formulation](path%20integral%20formulation.md)}@} extending {@{the [principle of least action](principle%20of%20least%20action.md) to [quantum mechanics](quantum%20mechanics.md) for [electrons](electrons.md) and [photons](photons.md)}@}. In {@{this formulation}@}, particles {@{travel every possible path between the initial and final states}@}; {@{the probability of a specific final state}@} is obtained by {@{summing over all possible trajectories leading to it}@}. In {@{the classical regime}@}, {@{the path integral formulation}@} cleanly reproduces {@{Hamilton's principle, and [Fermat's principle](Fermat's%20principle.md) in [optics](optics.md)}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:35.510Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:35.510Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:52.555Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:52.555Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:51:23.211Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:51:23.211Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:31:22.684Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:31:22.684Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:27:47.128Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:27:47.128Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:45.353Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:45.353Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:12:15.487Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:12:15.487Z!fsrs,2026-08-23T20:40:44.382Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-15T20:40:44.382Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:27:22.134Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:27:22.134Z-->

### classical field theory

In {@{Lagrangian mechanics}@}, {@{the generalized coordinates}@} form {@{a discrete set of variables that define the configuration of a system}@}. In {@{[classical field theory](classical%20field%20theory.md)}@}, {@{the physical system}@} is not {@{a set of discrete particles}@}, but rather {@{a continuous field _ϕ_\(__r__, _t_\) defined over a region of 3D space}@}. {@{Associated with the field}@} is {@{a [Lagrangian density](Lagrangian%20density.md) $${\mathcal {L} }(\phi ,\nabla \phi ,{\dot {\phi } },\mathbf {r} ,t)$$}@} defined in terms of {@{the field and its space and time derivatives at a location __r__ and time _t_}@}. Analogous to {@{the particle case}@}, for {@{non-relativistic applications the Lagrangian density}@} is also {@{the kinetic energy density of the field, minus its potential energy density}@} \(this is not {@{true in general, and the Lagrangian density has to be "reverse engineered"}@}\). {@{The Lagrangian}@} is then {@{the [volume integral](volume%20integral.md) of the Lagrangian density over 3D space}@} {@{$$L(t)=\int {\mathcal {L} }\,\mathrm {d} ^{3}\mathbf {r}$$}@} where {@{d<sup>3</sup>__r__ is a 3D [differential](total%20differential.md#differentials%20in%20several%20variables) [volume element](volume%20element.md)}@}. {@{The Lagrangian is a function of time}@} since the Lagrangian density has {@{implicit space dependence via the fields, and may have explicit spatial dependence}@}, but these are {@{removed in the integral}@}, leaving {@{only time in as the variable for the Lagrangian}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:49:55.028Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:49:55.028Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:09:20.398Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:09:20.398Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:00:57.375Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:00:57.375Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:40:28.921Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:40:28.921Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:30:56.048Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:30:56.048Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:48:31.333Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:48:31.333Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:56:32.339Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:56:32.339Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:34:24.969Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:34:24.969Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:57:08.200Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:57:08.200Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:00:56.631Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:00:56.631Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:49:59.475Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:49:59.475Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:28:06.390Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:28:06.390Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:27:59.452Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:27:59.452Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T18:08:34.784Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T18:08:34.784Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:48.812Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:48.812Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:30:06.291Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:30:06.291Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:25:51.256Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:25:51.256Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:10.509Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:10.509Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:03:08.832Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:03:08.832Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:56:17.918Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:56:17.918Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:55:05.192Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:55:05.192Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:42:30.500Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:42:30.500Z-->

### Noether's theorem

{@{The action principle, and the Lagrangian formalism}@}, are tied closely to {@{[Noether's theorem](Noether's%20theorem.md)}@}, which connects {@{physical [conserved quantities](conserved%20quantity.md) to continuous [symmetries](symmetry%20(physics).md) of a physical system}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:52:26.328Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:52:26.328Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T23:36:07.489Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T23:36:07.489Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T22:24:47.699Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T22:24:47.699Z-->

If {@{the Lagrangian is invariant under a symmetry}@}, then {@{the resulting equations of motion are also invariant under that symmetry}@}. {@{This characteristic}@} is very helpful in showing that {@{theories are consistent with either [special relativity](special%20relativity.md) or [general relativity](general%20relativity.md)}@}. <!--SR:!fsrs,2026-08-25T01:02:32.220Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T01:02:32.220Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T06:50:38.442Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T06:50:38.442Z!fsrs,2026-08-25T00:29:52.206Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-17T00:29:52.206Z!fsrs,2026-08-24T17:21:18.143Z,8,8.2956,1,2,1,0,0,2026-08-16T17:21:18.143Z-->

## see also

> ___![This is a mosaic image, one of the largest ever taken by NASA's Hubble Space Telescope, of the [Crab Nebula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crab_Nebula), a six-light-year-wide expanding remnant of a star's supernova explosion. Japanese and Chinese astronomers recorded this violent event in 1054 CE. <br/> The orange filaments are the tattered remains of the star and consist mostly of hydrogen. The rapidly spinning neutron star embedded in the center of the nebula is the dynamo powering the nebula's eerie interior bluish glow. The blue light comes from electrons whirling at nearly the speed of light around magnetic field lines from the neutron star. The neutron star, like a lighthouse, ejects twin beams of radiation that appear to pulse 30 times a second due to the neutron star's rotation. A neutron star is the crushed ultra-dense core of the exploded star. <p> <br/> The Crab Nebula derived its name from its appearance in a drawing made by Irish astronomer Lord Rosse in 1844, using a 36-inch telescope. When viewed by Hubble, as well as by large ground-based telescopes such as the European Southern Observatory's Very Large Telescope, the Crab Nebula takes on a more detailed appearance that yields clues into the spectacular demise of a star, 6,500 light-years away. <p> The newly composed image was assembled from 24 individual Wide Field and Planetary Camera 2 exposures taken in October 1999, January 2000, and December 2000. The colors in the image indicate the different elements that were expelled during the explosion. Blue in the filaments in the outer part of the nebula represents neutral oxygen, green is singly-ionized sulfur, and red indicates doubly-ionized oxygen.](../../archives/Wikimedia%20Commons/Crab%20Nebula.jpg) [Astronomy portal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portal:Astronomy)___

## footnotes

1. (annotation: notation for {@{component-wise differentiation}@}) Sometimes in this context {@{the [variational derivative](variational%20derivative.md)}@} denoted and defined as {@{$${\frac {\delta }{\delta \mathbf {r} _{k} } }\equiv {\frac {\partial }{\partial \mathbf {r} _{k} } }-{\frac {\mathrm {d} }{\mathrm {d} t} }{\frac {\partial }{\partial {\dot {\mathbf {r} } }_{k} } }$$}@} is used. Throughout {@{this article}@} only {@{partial and total derivatives are used}@}. <a id="^nb-1"></a>^nb-1
2. (annotation: {@{d'Alembert's principle}@}) Here {@{the virtual displacements}@} are {@{assumed reversible}@}, it is possible for {@{some systems to have non-reversible virtual displacements that violate this principle}@}, see {@{[Udwadia–Kalaba equation](Udwadia–Kalaba%20equation.md)}@}. <a id="^nb-2"></a>^nb-2
3. (annotation: {@{individual constraint force does no virtual work}@}) In other words {@{$$\mathbf {C} _{k}\cdot \delta \mathbf {r} _{k}=0$$}@} for {@{particle _k_ subject to a constraint force}@}, however {@{$$C_{k\,x}\delta x_{k}\neq 0,\quad C_{k\,y}\delta y_{k}\neq 0,\quad C_{k\,z}\delta z_{k}\neq 0$$}@} because of {@{the constraint equations on the __r__<sub>_k_</sub> coordinates}@}. (annotation: That is, {@{only the dot product}@} is {@{guaranteed to be zero}@}.) <a id="^nb-3"></a>^nb-3
4. (annotation: {@{two-body central force problem}@}) {@{The Lagrangian}@} also can be written {@{explicitly for a rotating frame}@}.
